, Proposition A.10]
(inspired by David Rydh) proving that
Id\to p_{\ast}p^{\ast}
is an isomorphism holds without assuming étale descent. Suppose that
\mathcal{E}
is a locally free sheaf, and consider a cover of
\mathcal{X}
by smooth morphisms with Nisnevich-local sections
f_{i}:X_{i}\to\mathcal{X}
from schemes. Using some Zariski refinement, one can suppose that
f_{i}^{\ast}(\mathcal{E})
is free. By conservativity, it suffices to prove the claim for
\mathbb{V}_{X_{i}}(f_{i}^{\ast}(\mathcal{E}))
, but this follows from the homotopy axiom. Hence, the claim is true if
\mathcal{E}
is a locally free sheaf. The rest of the proof of
[
Kha19
, Proposition A.10]
use only conservativity along the smooth surjective morphism:
\sigma:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E}[-1])
, which admit a section, hence the arguments extends to our case. The same arguments shows that the unit
p_{!}p^{!}\to Id
is an isomorphism. Notice that, in
[
Kha19
]
, Khan works with six functors extended to derived stacks, but coefficient systems depends only on the classical truncation as said above, hence the same arguments applies in our non-derived setting.

Consider now an affine bundle
\pi:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
modeled on
\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})
. Consider the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})\times_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

The left square is Cartesian because is is obtained by applying the isomorphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})\times_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})\times_{\mathcal{X}}\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})\times_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})\times_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{p}\times_{\mathcal{X}}Id_{\mathcal{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Id_{\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})}\times\mu}

to the Cartesian diagram for
\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})\times_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{Y}
. The right square is Cartesian because is is obtained by applying
\sigma:\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})\times_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{Y}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{Y}
(which is assumed to be an isomorphism) to the Cartesian diagram for
\mathcal{Y}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{Y}
. From
[
AP24
, Lemma 3.5]
,
\mu
is canonically equivalent with
\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E})\to\mathcal{Y}
and then
Id\to\mu_{\ast}\mu^{\ast}
is an isomorphism. By smooth base change, we have:

(2.27)

\displaystyle\pi^{\ast}(Id\to\pi_{\ast}\pi^{\ast})\simeq(Id\to\mu_{\ast}\mu^{\ast})\pi^{\ast}

which is an isomorphism, and
\pi
is smooth from Lemma
2.4
. Then, if the coefficient system satisfies étale descent, or if
\pi
admits Nisnevich-local sections,
\pi^{\ast}
is conservative, and then
Id\to\pi_{\ast}\pi^{\ast}
is an isomorphism. The proof that
\pi_{!}\pi^{!}\to Id
is an isomorphism is similar.

∎

2.2.2.
Thom twists and stability

This is a version for stacks of well known results of Ayoub
[
Ayo07a
, Section 1.5]
:

Lemma 2.7
.

Consider a quasi-coherent complex
\mathcal{E}
on
\mathcal{X}
which is perfect with amplitude in
[0,1]
. Consider the smooth morphism
p:\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})\to\mathcal{X}
, and its zero section
s:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})
. The adjoint pair of functors:

(2.28)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\mathcal{E}}:=p_{\#}s_{!}:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X})\rightleftharpoons\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X}):s^{!}p^{\ast}=:\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}}

forms inverse equivalence, called Thom equivalences. Given an exact triangle of quasi-coherent complex
\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{X}
which are perfect with amplitude in
[0,1]
, there are a natural isomorphism
\Sigma^{\mathcal{F}}\simeq\Sigma^{\mathcal{E}}\circ\Sigma^{\mathcal{G}}
and
\Sigma^{-\mathcal{F}}\simeq\Sigma^{-\mathcal{G}}\circ\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}}
.

Proof.

Given an exact triangle of quasi-coherent complex
\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{X}
which are perfect with amplitude in
[0,1]
, consider the following commutative diagram of stacks:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\mathcal{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathcal{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathcal{E}}}

the square is Cartesian because the corresponding square is a pushout in
{\rm QCoh}(\mathcal{X})
. Notice that the cotangent complex of
q
is
(p_{\mathcal{F}})^{\ast}(\mathcal{G})
, hence
q
is smooth. The exchange isomorphisms (
2.1.2
) gives natural isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle:=(p_{\mathcal{F}})_{\#}(s_{\mathcal{F}})_{!}\simeq(p_{\mathcal{E}})_{\#}q_{\#}p_{!}(s_{\mathcal{G}})_{!}\simeq(p_{\mathcal{E}})_{\#}(s_{\mathcal{E}})_{!}(p_{\mathcal{G}})_{\#}(s_{\mathcal{G}})_{!}=:\Sigma^{\mathcal{E}}\circ\Sigma^{\mathcal{G}}

(2.29)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{-\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle:=(s_{\mathcal{F}})^{!}(p_{\mathcal{F}})^{\ast}\simeq(s_{\mathcal{G}})^{!}p^{!}q^{\ast}(p_{\mathcal{E}})^{\ast}\simeq(s_{\mathcal{G}})^{!}(p_{\mathcal{G}})^{\ast}(s_{\mathcal{E}})^{!}(p_{\mathcal{E}})^{\ast}=:\Sigma^{-\mathcal{G}}\circ\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}}

where the second isomorphism of both lines is the isomorphism from (
2.1.2
). These isomorphisms are compatible with the unit and counit of the adjunction (
2.28
), hence
\Sigma^{\pm\mathcal{F}}
are inverse isomorphisms if an only if
\Sigma^{\pm\mathcal{E}}
and
\Sigma^{\pm\mathcal{G}}
are. Hence it suffices to prove that for
\Sigma^{\pm\mathcal{E}}
and
\Sigma^{\pm\mathcal{E}[-1]}
, for
\mathcal{E}
a locally free sheaf.

Consider a locally free sheaf
\mathcal{E}
. As in
[
CD24
, Lemma 4.1]
, using a cover of
\mathcal{X}
by smooth morphisms with Nisnevich-local sections
q_{i}:X_{i}\to\mathcal{X}
by qcqs schemes where
(q_{i})^{\ast}(\mathcal{E})
is trivial, we obtain from the stability axiom that
\Sigma^{\pm\mathcal{E}}
are inverse isomorphisms. Now, from the exact triangle
\mathcal{E}[-1]\to 0\to\mathcal{E}
, one get isomorphisms
Id\simeq\Sigma^{0}\simeq\Sigma^{\mathcal{E}[-1]}\circ\Sigma^{\mathcal{E}}
and
Id\simeq\Sigma^{0}\simeq\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}}\circ\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}[-1]}
. These isomorphisms are compatible with the adjunctions, in the sense that:

\displaystyle Id

\displaystyle\to\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}}\circ\Sigma^{\mathcal{E}}\to\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}}\circ\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}[-1]}\circ\Sigma^{\mathcal{E}[-1]}\circ\Sigma^{\mathcal{E}}\simeq Id

(2.30)

\displaystyle Id

\displaystyle\simeq\Sigma^{\mathcal{E}[-1]}\circ\Sigma^{\mathcal{E}}\circ\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}}\circ\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}[-1]}\to\Sigma^{\mathcal{E}[-1]}\circ\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}[-1]}\to Id

are the identity. But the morphisms
Id\to\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}}\circ\Sigma^{\mathcal{E}}
and
\Sigma^{\mathcal{E}}\to\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}}\to Id
are isomorphisms as proven above, hence the two morphisms above are isomorphisms, and
\Sigma^{\pm\mathcal{E}}
are isomorphisms, hence
Id\to\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}[-1]}\circ\Sigma^{\mathcal{E}[-1]}
and
\Sigma^{\mathcal{E}[-1]}\circ\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}[-1]}\to Id
are isomorphisms.
∎

This is an analogue of
[
Ayo07b
, Proposition 3.1.7]
:

Lemma 2.8
.

Consider a specialization system
{\rm sp}:\mathcal{D}_{1}\to\mathcal{D}_{2}
over
\eta\to B\leftarrow\sigma
. Given a stack
f:\mathcal{X}\to B
and a perfect complex of amplitude
[0,1]
, the natural morphisms:

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}}{\rm sp}_{f}:=(p_{\sigma})_{\#}(s_{\sigma})_{!}{\rm sp}_{f}

\displaystyle\to{\rm sp}_{f}(p_{\eta})_{\#}(s_{\eta})_{!}=:{\rm sp}_{f}\Sigma^{\mathcal{E}_{\eta}}

(2.31)

\displaystyle{\rm sp}_{f}\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}_{\eta}}:={\rm sp}_{f}(s_{\eta})^{!}(p_{\eta})^{\ast}

\displaystyle\to(s_{\sigma})^{!}(p_{\sigma})^{\ast}{\rm sp}_{f}=:\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}}{\rm sp}_{f}

are inverse isomorphisms, compatible with the isomorphisms coming from short exact sequences.

Proof.

From the compatibility between specialization and adjunctions, these morphisms are compatible with the isomorphisms
Id\simeq\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}}\circ\Sigma^{\mathcal{E}}
and
\Sigma^{\mathcal{E}}\simeq\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}}\to Id
, hence it suffices to prove that the morphism for
\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}}
is an isomorphism. Moreover, from the compatibility between specialization systems and composition and base change, these morphisms are compatible with the isomorphisms coming from short exact sequences. Using the same trick that in the proof of Lemma
2.7
, it suffices to show that for
\mathcal{E}
a locally free sheaf. Consider a cover of
\mathcal{X}
by smooth morphisms with Nisnevich-local sections
q_{i}:X_{i}\to\mathcal{X}
by qcqs schemes where
(q_{i})^{\ast}(\mathcal{E})
is trivial. Using commutation between specialization systems and smooth pullbacks, with compositions of
\ast
-pullbacks, and with the exchange morphism
Ex^{!\ast}
(obtained by adjointness from the compatibility with
Ex^{\ast}_{!}
), we obtain that:

(2.32)

\displaystyle(q_{i,\sigma})^{\ast}({\rm sp}_{f}\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}_{\eta}}\to\Sigma^{-\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}}{\rm sp}_{f})

is isomorphic with:

(2.33)

\displaystyle({\rm sp}_{f\circ q_{i}}\Sigma^{-((q_{i})^{\ast}(\mathcal{E}))_{\eta}}\to\Sigma^{-((q_{i})^{\ast}(\mathcal{E}))_{\sigma}}{f\circ q_{i}})(q_{i,\eta})^{\ast}

hence, by conservativity of
(q_{i})^{\ast}
, it suffices to prove it for
\mathcal{E}
trivial on a qcqs scheme
X
. Using the compatibility with exact sequence, it suffices to prove it for
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{O}_{X}
, i.e. for
p:\mathbb{A}^{1}_{X}\to X
and
s:X\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{X}
. This case is proven in
[
Ayo07b
, Proposition 3.1.7]
.
∎

In particular, Thom twists commutes with base change, hence for any morphism
g
, we have natural isomorphisms:

\displaystyle g_{!,\ast}\Sigma^{\pm g^{\ast}(\mathcal{E})}

\displaystyle\simeq\Sigma^{\pm\mathcal{E}}g_{!,\ast}

(2.34)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\pm g^{\ast}(\mathcal{E})}g^{!,\ast}

\displaystyle\simeq g^{!,\ast}\Sigma^{\pm\mathcal{E}}

compatible with composition of
g
, and composition of Thom twists. This is contained in
[
Ayo07a
, Scholie 1.4.2 2)]
in the scheme case.

2.2.3.
Proper and smooth morphism

We recall here the construction of the natural morphism
f_{!}\to f_{\ast}
, for
f
separated, and the purity isomorphism. The main point of giving explicitly this construction is to prove the compatibility between these morphisms and specialization systems. The main idea of the construction is the diagonal trick. For
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
, consider the following diagram with Cartesian square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where
\Delta_{f}
is the diagonal of
f
. Suppose we have an isomorphism
(\Delta_{f})_{!}\simeq(\Delta_{f})_{\ast}
(resp.
(\Delta_{f})^{!}\simeq(\Delta_{f})^{\ast}
). Then one obtains a natural morphism:

\displaystyle f_{!}

\displaystyle\simeq f_{!}(p_{1})_{\ast}(\Delta_{f})_{\ast}\overset{Ex_{!\ast}}{\to}f_{\ast}(p_{2})_{!}(\Delta_{f})_{\ast}\simeq f_{\ast}(p_{2})_{!}(\Delta_{f})_{!}\simeq f_{\ast}

(2.35)

\displaystyle f^{!}

\displaystyle\simeq(\Delta_{f})^{\ast}(p_{2})^{\ast}f^{!}\simeq(\Delta_{f})^{!}(p_{2})^{\ast}f^{!}\overset{Ex^{\ast!}}{\to}(\Delta_{f})^{!}(p_{1})^{!}f^{\ast}\simeq f^{\ast}

This procedure can be applied recursively:

•

If
f
is a monomorphism,
\Delta_{f}=Id
, hence one obtains
f_{!}\to f_{\ast}
, which is an isomorphism if
f
is a closed immersion (this can be checked at the scheme level).

•

If
f
is separated and representable by algebraic spaces,
\Delta_{f}
is an closed immersion, hence one obtains a morphism
f_{!}\to f_{\ast}
, which is an isomorphism if
f
is proper (and representable by algebraic spaces) (this can be checked at the level of algebraic spaces, where it follows from
[
Kha21
, Theorem 2.34 ii)]
).

•

If
f
is separated (possibly not representable),
\Delta_{f}
is proper and representable by algebraic spaces, hence one obtains a morphism
f_{!}\to f_{\ast}
.

Similarly:

•

If
f
is a monomorphism,
\Delta_{f}=Id
, hence one obtains
f^{!}\to f^{\ast}
, which is an isomorphism if
f
is an open immersion (this can be checked at the scheme level).

•

If
f
is étale,
\Delta_{f}
is an open immersion, hence one obtains a morphism
f^{!}\to f^{\ast}
, which is an isomorphism (this can be checked at the scheme level).

The construction of the purity isomorphism in the stack case was described in
[
CD24
, Section 4]
, following Ayoub
[
Ayo07a
, Section 1.6]
in the scheme case. Notice that the authors of
[
CD24
]
works with motivic stable homotopy, but one checks directly that the arguments of
[
CD24
, Section 4]
works for any coefficient system extended to stacks using descent along smooth morphisms with Nisnevich-local sections as in
[
Kha25
, Corollary 7.2.4]
. As seen above, for
f
smooth, one obtains:

(2.36)

\displaystyle(\Delta_{f})^{!}(p_{2})^{\ast}f^{!}\to f^{\ast}

From
[
CD24
, Theorem 4.24]
(by passing to the adjoint), this morphism is an isomorphism. From
[
AP24
, Theorem 1.5]
,
\Delta_{f}
, which is representable by algebraic spaces, admits a deformation to the normal cone, which is a
1
-stack. Namely, there is a commutative diagram with Cartesian squares:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\Delta_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{L}_{f})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

where the vertical arrows from the second to the third line are smooth. Using base change, we obtain natural morphisms:

(2.37)

\displaystyle(\Delta_{f})^{!}(p_{2})^{\ast}\leftarrow\pi_{\ast}\tilde{s}^{!}\tilde{p}^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}\rightarrow s^{!}p^{\ast}=:\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{f}}

More precisely, they are given by:

\displaystyle\pi_{\ast}\tilde{s}^{!}\tilde{p}^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}\to\pi_{\ast}\tilde{s}^{!}\tilde{1}_{\ast}\tilde{1}^{\ast}\tilde{p}^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}\simeq\pi_{\ast}\tilde{s}^{!}\tilde{1}_{\ast}(p_{2})^{\ast}\simeq\pi_{\ast}1_{\ast}(\Delta_{f})^{!}(p_{2})^{\ast}\simeq(\Delta_{f})^{!}(p_{2})^{\ast}

(2.38)

\displaystyle\pi_{\ast}\tilde{s}^{!}\tilde{p}^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}\to\pi_{\ast}\tilde{s}^{!}\tilde{0}_{\ast}\tilde{0}^{\ast}\tilde{p}^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}\simeq\pi_{\ast}\tilde{s}^{!}\tilde{0}_{\ast}p^{\ast}\simeq\pi_{\ast}0_{\ast}s^{!}p^{\ast}\simeq s^{!}p^{\ast}

From
[
CD24
, Corollary 4.25]
(by passing to the adjoint), those two morphisms are isomorphisms, which gives an isomorphism, called the isomorphism of relative purity:

(2.39)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{f}}\circ f^{!}\simeq f^{\ast}

We now prove compatibility between the morphisms introduced above and specialization systems.

Lemma 2.9
.

Given any specialization system
{\rm sp}:\mathcal{D}_{1}\to\mathcal{D}_{2}
over
\eta\to B\leftarrow\sigma
, and a separated map
g:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
, the following square is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{\sigma})_{!}{\rm sp}_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}_{f\circ g}(g_{\eta})_{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{\sigma})_{\ast}{\rm sp}_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}_{f\circ g}(g_{\eta})_{\ast}}

if
f
is proper and representable by algebraic spaces, it is a commutative square of isomorphisms. If
g
is a monomorphism, the following square is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}_{f\circ g}(g_{\eta})^{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{\sigma})^{!}{\rm sp}_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}_{f\circ g}(g_{\eta})^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{\sigma})^{\ast}{\rm sp}_{f}}

and it is a square of isomorphisms if
g
is open.

Proof.

We show the first part of the Lemma, the second part being formally similar. We show this inductively. If
f
is the identity, all the morphisms are the identity, hence the square is trivially a square of isomorphisms. Suppose we have proven that the square of the Lemma for
\Delta_{f}
is a square of isomorphisms. We can divide the square of the Lemma for
f
into the diagram of morphisms (where we have removed the subscripts for readability):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}f_{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}(p_{1})_{\ast}(\Delta_{f})_{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}(p_{1})_{\ast}{\rm sp}(\Delta_{f})_{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}{\rm sp}(p_{1})_{\ast}(\Delta_{f})_{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}f_{!}(p_{1})_{\ast}(\Delta_{f})_{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\ast}(p_{2})_{!}(\Delta_{f})_{!}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\ast}(p_{2})_{!}{\rm sp}(\Delta_{f})_{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\ast}{\rm sp}(p_{2})_{!}(\Delta_{f})_{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}f_{\ast}(p_{2})_{!}(\Delta_{f})_{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}f_{\ast}}

the upper left and lower right triangle are commutative from the compatibility of specialization systems with composition. The central left square is commutative because the square of the Lemma for
\Delta_{f}
is a square of isomorphisms. The central right rectangle is commutative because it expresses the compatibility between specialization systems and the exchange morphism
Ex_{!\ast}
, which is obtained by adjunction from the compatibility between specialization systems and
Ex^{\ast}_{!}
. The upper right and lower left trapezoids commutes because they are formed by applying independent functors. Hence the outer square commutes. If
g
is proper and representable, by assumption
g_{!}{\rm sp}\to{\rm sp}g_{!}
is an isomorphism, hence the square is a square of isomorphism. By recursion, one obtains that the square of the lemma commutes for
g
separated, and is a square of isomorphisms for
g
proper and representable.
∎

Lemma 2.10
.

Given any specialization system
{\rm sp}:\mathcal{D}_{1}\to\mathcal{D}_{2}
over
\eta\to B\leftarrow\sigma
, and a smooth map
g:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
, the following square is a commutative square of isomorphisms:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}_{f\circ g}\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{g_{\sigma}}}(g_{\sigma})^{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{g_{\eta}}}(g_{\eta})^{!}{\rm sp}_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}_{f\circ g}(g_{\eta})^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{\sigma})^{\ast}{\rm sp}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Proof.

By assumption, the lower horizontal morphism is an isomorphism, hence the commutativity will imply that the upper horizontal morphism is an isomorphism. Consider the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}\tilde{s}^{!}\tilde{p}^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}\tilde{s}^{!}{\rm sp}\tilde{p}^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}\pi_{\ast}\tilde{s}^{!}\tilde{p}^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}\tilde{s}^{!}0_{\ast}p^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}\tilde{s}^{!}0_{\ast}{\rm sp}p^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}\tilde{s}^{!}{\rm sp}0_{\ast}p^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}\pi_{\ast}\tilde{s}^{!}0_{\ast}p^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{!}p^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{!}{\rm sp}p^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}s^{!}p^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where the upper left horizontal arrow is an isomorphism because
\tilde{p},\pi
are smooth, the left and right vertical long arrows are isomorphisms from the proof of relative purity, the central horizontal arrow is an isomorphism because
0
is a closed immersion, using Lemma
2.9
, and the lower right horizontal arrow is an isomorphism from Lemma
2.8
. The upper right and lower left squares are commutative because they comes from applying independent functors, the upper left (resp lower right) rectangle is commutative from the compatibility of specialization systems with the exchange morphism
Ex^{\ast}_{\ast}
(resp
Ex^{!}_{\ast}
). Hence, by diagram chase, the whole diagram is a commutative diagram of isomorphism, in particular, the upper right horizontal arrow is an isomorphism. We can now write the same diagram, replacing
0
by
1
,
s
by
\Delta_{f}
and
p
by
p_{2}
, to obtain a commutative diagram of isomorphisms:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}\tilde{s}^{!}\tilde{p}^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}\pi_{\ast}\tilde{s}^{!}\tilde{p}^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta_{f})^{!}(p_{2})^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}(\Delta_{f})^{!}(p_{2})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Now, consider the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta_{f})^{!}(p_{2})^{\ast}f^{!}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta_{f})^{!}(p_{2})^{\ast}{\rm sp}f^{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta_{f})^{!}{\rm sp}(p_{2})^{\ast}f^{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}(\Delta_{f})^{!}(p_{2})^{\ast}f^{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta_{f})^{!}(p_{1})^{!}f^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta_{f})^{!}(p_{1})^{!}{\rm sp}f^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta_{f})^{!}{\rm sp}(p_{1})^{!}f^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}(\Delta_{f})^{!}(p_{1})^{!}f^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}f^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where the upper left horizontal arrow is an isomorphism as proven above, and the arrows from the first to the second line, obtained from the exchange morphism
Ex^{\ast!}
are isomorphisms because
f
is smooth. The upper right and lower left squares trivially commutes, the lower right triangle commutes from the compatibility of specialization systems with compositions, and the upper left rectangles commutes from the compatibility of specialization systems with
Ex^{\ast!}
. A simple diagram chase show then that this diagram is a commutative diagram of isomorphisms. By the construction of the purity isomorphism, the diagram of the lemma is obtained by gluing vertically the three above diagram, hence is a commutative diagram of isomorphisms.
∎

We prove compatibility with composition:

Lemma 2.11
.

The morphism
f_{!}\to f_{\ast}
for separated
f
is compatible with composition. The relative purity isomorphism is compatible with composition, i.e. for
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
and
g:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{Z}
two smooth morphisms, the following square of isomorphisms is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{f}}f^{!}\circ\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{g}}g^{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\circ g^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{g\circ f}}(g\circ f)^{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g\circ f)^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where the left vertical arrow comes from the cofiber sequence
f^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{g})\to\mathbb{L}_{g\circ f}\to\mathbb{L}_{f}
.

Proof.

Consider the following commutative diagram with Cartesian square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}\times_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}\times_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}\times_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathcal{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{g\circ f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f^{\ast}g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}

To prove the claim that the morphism
f_{!}\to f_{\ast}
for separated
f
is compatible with composition, one can proceed recursively. We show how to adapt the proof of
[
Ayo07a
, Proposition 1.7.3]
. If
f
and
g
are isomorphisms, this is obvious. Suppose that we have proven compatibility with composition for a class
E
of proper and representable morphisms that is stable by base change and composition (e.g., isomorphisms, or monomorphisms, or closed immersions, or proper and representable morphisms). Consider now the class
E^{\prime}\supset E
of morphisms whose diagonal is in
E
. This class is stable by base change, and it is also compatible with composition, from the above diagram. From Lemma
2.9
, the morphism
f_{!}\to f_{\ast}
for
f\in E^{\prime}
is compatible with base change. Then the arguments of
[
Ayo07a
, Proposition 1.7.3]
carry on directly to show compatibility with composition for morphisms of
E^{\prime}
.

we consider now the compatibility of the purity isomorphism with composition, for smooth
f,g
. Consider the following square of isomorphisms:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta_{f})^{!}(p_{2})^{\ast}f^{!}\circ(\Delta_{g})^{!}(p_{2})^{\ast}g^{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\circ g^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta_{g\circ f})^{!}(p_{2})^{\ast}(g\circ f)^{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g\circ f)^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where the left vertical arrows comes from smooth base change (
2.1.2
) in the above diagram, and the horizontal arrows from the purity isomorphisms for
f,g
and
g\circ f
. This diagram is the dual of those of
[
CD24
, Proposition 3.23]
, which is proven here (adapting the arguments of
[
Ayo07a
, Proposition 1.7.3]
) to be commutative for
f
and
gf
representable. This restriction arise there because
[
CD24
, Proposition 4.23]
is a preliminary result to show the relative purity, which is used afterward to prove smooth base change (
2.1.2
). Once one knows that smooth base change holds, the arguments of
[
CD24
, Proposition 4.23]
applies directly to our case, giving that the above diagram is commutative.

Using deformation to the normal cone, we obtain a commutative diagram with Cartesian squares over
\mathbb{A}^{1}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\Delta_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\Delta_{g\circ f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\Delta_{f^{\ast}g}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\Delta_{g}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{s}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{s}_{g\circ f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{p}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{p}_{g\circ f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{s}_{f^{\ast}g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{p}_{f^{\ast}g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{s}_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{p}_{g}}

whose restriction over
1
is the above diagram, and whose restriction over
0
is:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{L}_{f})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{L}_{g\circ f})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{X}}(f^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{g}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbb{L}_{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{g\circ f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{g\circ f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{f^{\ast}g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{f^{\ast}g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{g}}

Using base change, we obtain the following commutative diagram of isomorphisms:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta_{f})^{!}(p_{2})^{\ast}\circ f^{!}\circ(\Delta_{g})^{!}(p_{2})^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}(\tilde{s}_{f})^{!}(\tilde{p}_{f})^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}\circ f^{!}\circ\pi_{\ast}(\tilde{s}_{g})^{!}(\tilde{p}_{g})^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s_{f})^{!}(p_{f})^{\ast}\circ f^{!}\circ(s_{g})^{!}(p_{g})^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta_{g\circ f})^{!}(p_{2})^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}(\tilde{s}_{g\circ f})^{!}(\tilde{p}_{g\circ f})^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s_{g\circ f})^{!}(p_{g\circ f})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where the right vertical arrow is by definition
\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{f}}f^{!}\circ\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{g}}\to\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{g\circ f}}
, and the horizontal arrows are the roofs (
2.37
) for
f,g
and
g\circ f
. By construction of the purity isomorphism, the result follows.
∎

2.2.4.
A contraction lemma

Consider a stack
\mathcal{X}
, a closed immersion
i:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{X}
, and its open complement
j:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{X}
. Using the unit
j_{!}j^{\ast}\to Id
,
Id\to i_{\ast}i^{\ast}
and the isomorphisms
j^{!}\simeq j^{\ast}
,
i_{!}\simeq i_{\ast}
, one obtains:

(2.40)

\displaystyle j_{!}j^{\ast}\to Id\to i_{!}i^{\ast}

one can check using a cover by smooth morphisms with Nisnevich-local sections that
(j^{\ast},i^{\ast})
are jointly conservative. By base change,
j^{\ast}i_{!}\simeq 0
, hence the arguments of
[
Del01
, Lemma 1.1]
shows that the above extends uniquely to a distinguished triangle:

(2.41)

\displaystyle j_{!}j^{\ast}\to Id\to i_{!}i^{\ast}\to j_{!}j^{\ast}[1]

Lemma 2.12
.

Given a specialization system
{\rm sp}:\mathcal{D}_{1}\to\mathcal{D}_{2}
over
\eta\to B\leftarrow\sigma
, and an open-closed decomposition
(j,i)
, the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(j_{\sigma})_{!}(j_{\sigma})^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{\sigma})_{!}(i_{\sigma})^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j_{\sigma})_{!}(j_{\sigma})^{\ast}[1]{\rm sp}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}(j_{\eta})_{!}(j_{\eta})^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}(i_{\eta})_{!}(i_{\eta})^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm sp}(j_{\eta})_{!}(j_{\eta})^{\ast}[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

Proof.

The leftmost part can be decomposed into:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}j^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}j^{!}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\ast}i^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{!}i^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}{\rm sp}j^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}{\rm sp}j^{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\ast}{\rm sp}i^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{!}{\rm sp}i^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}j_{!}j^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}j_{!}j^{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}i_{\ast}i^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}i_{!}i^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

the upper left and lower right squares commutes respectively from Lemma
2.9
, the upper right and lower left squares trivially commutes, and the two central triangles commutes from adjunctions. To show that the right square of the diagram of the Lemma commutes, we can argue as in
[
Del01
, Lemma 1.1]
. Namely, consider the morphism
(i_{\sigma})_{!}(i_{\sigma})^{\ast}{\rm sp}\to{\rm sp}(i_{\eta})_{!}(i_{\eta})^{\ast}
obtained from the cone of the two leftmost vertical arrows of the diagram of the Lemma, and the difference
w
with the third vertical arrow of the diagram of the Lemma: we have to show
w=0
. The composite arrow:

(2.42)

\displaystyle{\rm sp}\to(i_{\sigma})_{!}(i_{\sigma})^{\ast}{\rm sp}\overset{w}{\to}{\rm sp}(i_{\eta})_{!}(i_{\eta})^{\ast}

has to vanish, as the second square commutes. Then
w
factorize through a map
(j_{\sigma})_{!}(j_{\sigma})^{\ast}[1]{\rm sp}\to{\rm sp}(i_{\eta})_{!}(i_{\eta})^{\ast}
, which corresponds by adjunction to a map:

(2.43)

\displaystyle(j_{\sigma})^{\ast}[1]{\rm sp}\to(j_{\sigma})^{\ast}{\rm sp}(i_{\eta})_{!}(i_{\eta})^{\ast}\simeq{\rm sp}(j_{\eta})^{\ast}(i_{\eta})_{!}(i_{\eta})^{\ast}\simeq 0

hence
w=0
.
∎

Consider now the closed immersion
0:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}
, and the projection
\pi:\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathcal{X}
. There is a natural morphism
\pi_{\ast}\to\pi_{\ast}0_{\ast}0^{\ast}\simeq 0^{\ast}
. The first part of the following lemma is a well known consequence of the localization and homotopy property. We are particularly interested in the second part, expressing the compatibility with specialization systems.

Lemma 2.13
.

Consider the morphism
\bar{q}:\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\to[\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
. On the image of
\bar{q}^{\ast}
, the morphism
\pi_{\ast}\to 0^{\ast}
is an isomorphism. For any specialization system, on the image of
(\bar{q}_{\eta})^{\ast}
, the following:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{\sigma})_{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}(\pi_{\eta})_{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0_{\sigma})^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}(0_{\eta})^{\ast}}

is a square of isomorphisms.

Proof.

Consider the commutative and Cartesian square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{G}_{m,\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{j}}

where
\tilde{j}
is an open immersion, and
\bar{q}\circ j=\tilde{j}\circ\pi\circ j
. The composition of morphisms:

(2.44)

\displaystyle Id\to\pi_{\ast}\pi^{\ast}\to\pi_{\ast}0_{\ast}0^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}\simeq(\pi\circ 0)_{\ast}(0\circ\pi)^{\ast}

is identified with the unit of the adjunction for
0\circ\pi\simeq Id
, hence is an isomorphism. But
Id\to\pi_{\ast}\pi^{\ast}
is an isomorphism by the homotopy property, hence the second morphism is an isomorphism. In the localization exact triangle:

(2.45)

\displaystyle\pi_{\ast}j_{!}j^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}\to

\displaystyle\pi_{\ast}\pi^{\ast}\to\pi_{\ast}0_{!}0^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}

the second morphism is then an isomorphism, hence
\pi_{\ast}j_{!}j^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}\simeq 0
. In the localization exact triangle:

\displaystyle\pi_{\ast}j_{!}j^{\ast}\bar{q}^{\ast}\to

\displaystyle\pi_{\ast}\bar{q}^{\ast}\to 0^{\ast}\bar{q}^{\ast}

the first term is isomorphic with
\pi_{\ast}j_{!}j^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}\tilde{j}^{\ast}
, hence vanishes, and then the second arrow is an isomorphism, which proves the first part.

We check now the compatibility with specialization systems. The compositions:

\displaystyle{\rm sp}\simeq 0^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}{\rm sp}\to 0^{\ast}{\rm sp}\pi^{\ast}\to{\rm sp}0^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}\simeq{\rm sp}

(2.46)

\displaystyle{\rm sp}\simeq{\rm sp}\pi_{\ast}0_{\ast}\to\pi_{\ast}{\rm sp}0_{\ast}\to\pi_{\ast}0_{\ast}{\rm sp}\simeq{\rm sp}

are the identity, and the first (resp second) arrow is an isomorphism because
\pi
is smooth (resp
0
is a closed immersion), hence the second (resp first) arrow is an isomorphism too. Hence
0_{!}0^{\ast}{\rm sp}\pi^{\ast}\to{\rm sp}0_{!}0^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}
and
{\rm sp}\pi_{\ast}0_{!}0^{\ast}\to\pi_{\ast}{\rm sp}0_{!}0^{\ast}
are isomorphisms. Consider the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}j_{!}j^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}\pi^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}0_{!}0^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}j_{!}j^{\ast}{\rm sp}\pi^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}{\rm sp}\pi^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}0_{!}0^{\ast}{\rm sp}\pi^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}{\rm sp}j_{!}j^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}{\rm sp}\pi^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}{\rm sp}0_{!}0^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}\pi_{\ast}j_{!}j^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}\pi_{\ast}\pi^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}\pi_{\ast}0_{!}0^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where the morphisms from the second to third line forms morphisms of exact triangle from Lemma
2.12
. By the property of morphisms of exact triangles, the central left vertical arrow is an isomorphism, hence all the objects of the left column are trivial. Consider now the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}j_{!}j^{\ast}\bar{q}^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}\bar{q}^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}0_{!}0^{\ast}\bar{q}^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}j_{!}j^{\ast}{\rm sp}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}{\rm sp}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}0_{!}0^{\ast}{\rm sp}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}{\rm sp}j_{!}j^{\ast}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}{\rm sp}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}{\rm sp}0_{!}0^{\ast}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}\pi_{\ast}j_{!}j^{\ast}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}\pi_{\ast}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}\pi_{\ast}0_{!}0^{\ast}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where the morphisms from the second to third line forms morphisms of exact triangle from Lemma
2.12
. As proven above, all the objects of the left column are trivial, hence all the arrows from the central column to the right column are isomorphisms. By a diagram chase, the lower central and central right vertical arrows are isomorphisms. We obtain the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}{\rm sp}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}0_{!}0^{\ast}{\rm sp}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}0_{\ast}0^{\ast}{\rm sp}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0^{\ast}{\rm sp}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}0_{!}{\rm sp}0^{\ast}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}0_{\ast}{\rm sp}0^{\ast}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}0^{\ast}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}{\rm sp}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}{\rm sp}0_{!}0^{\ast}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}{\rm sp}0_{\ast}0^{\ast}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}\pi_{\ast}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}\pi_{\ast}0_{!}0^{\ast}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}\pi_{\ast}0_{\ast}0^{\ast}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}0^{\ast}\bar{q}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where the left squares commute by the above, the central square commute by Lemma
2.9
, the lower right rectangle commutes by functoriality of the exchange morphism, and the remaining ones trivially commutes. By diagram chase, we obtain that the outer square, which is the square of the Lemma, is a commutative square of isomorphisms.
∎

Corollary 2.14
.

On the image of
\bar{q}^{\ast}
, one obtains a natural morphism
0^{\ast}\simeq\pi_{\ast}\to 1^{\ast}
. For any specialization system, on the image of
(\bar{q}_{\eta})^{\ast}
, the following square is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0_{\sigma})^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}(0_{\eta})^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{\sigma})^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}(1_{\eta})^{\ast}}

Proof.

it suffices to check that the square is commutative. It is obtained by gluing vertically the commutative diagram of isomorphisms of Lemma
2.13
and the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}\pi_{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}1_{\ast}1^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}1_{\ast}{\rm sp}1^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ast}{\rm sp}1_{\ast}1^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}\pi_{\ast}1_{\ast}1^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}1^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where the upper right (resp lower left) triangle commutes from the compatibility of specialization systems with adjunctions (resp composition).

∎

3.
Hyperbolic localization for stacks

In this section, we work over an excellent quasi-separated algebraic space
S
. All our stacks are assumed to be quasi-separated, locally of finite presentation over
S
, with affine stabilizers.

3.1.
The Theta-correspondence

3.1.1.
The Theta-correspondence and Braden-Drinfeld-Gaitsgory theorem

Consider the algebraic stack
\Theta:=[\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
over
\mathbb{Z}
, where
\mathbb{G}_{m}
acts on
\mathbb{A}^{1}
with its usual action. We denote by
\Theta_{S}
its base change over
S
There is a natural diagram:

(3.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathbb{G}_{m,S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}

where the arrow
B\mathbb{G}_{m,S}\to\Theta_{S}
(resp.
S\to\Theta_{S}
) is given by the inclusion of
0
(resp.
1
), the arrow
S\to B\mathbb{G}_{m,S}
is the natural one, and the involution of
B\mathbb{G}_{m,S}
comes from the inversion
x\to x^{-1}
of
\mathbb{G}_{m,S}
(one use here the fact that
\mathbb{G}_{m,S}
is commutative).

Consider now a algebraic stack
\mathcal{X}
over
S
(as said above,
S
is assumed to be quasi-separated and excellent, and
\mathcal{X}
is assumed to be quasi-separated, locally of finite presentation over
S
, with affine stabilizers). Denote the mapping stacks:

\displaystyle{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})

\displaystyle={\rm Map}_{S}(\Theta_{S},\mathcal{X})

(3.2)

\displaystyle{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})

\displaystyle={\rm Map}_{S}(B\mathbb{G}_{m,S},\mathcal{X})

According to
[
AHR19
, Theorem 6.22]
(building on the results of
[
HLP14
]
),
{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})
and
{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})
are algebraic stacks, which are quasi-separated and locally of finite presentation over
S
, with affine stabilizers. One obtains morover from the above diagram a natural diagram of algebraic stacks (which are quasi-separated, locally of presentation, with affine stabilizer):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\iota}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

with
r
an involution,
\iota=\eta\circ\iota
and
p\circ\tilde{\iota}=Id
,
p
is of finite presentation, and
\eta
is representable by algebraic spaces.

We can now consider the hyperbolic localization functor:

(3.3)

\displaystyle p_{!}\eta^{\ast}:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathcal{D}({\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X}))

The following result is a generalization of the main theorem of Braden
[
Bra02
]
and Drinfeld-Gaitsgory
[
DG13
]
:

Theorem 3.1
.

Given a stack
\mathcal{X}
over
S
, one has a canonical adjunction:

(3.4)

\displaystyle p_{!}\eta^{\ast}:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X})\rightleftharpoons\mathcal{D}({\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})):\eta_{!}(r\circ p)^{\ast}

We will prove this in the next section, following the proof of Drinfeld-Gaitsgory
[
DG13
]
,
[
Dri13
]
.

3.1.2.
The quotient case

Consider an algebraic space
X
(as always, assumed to be quasi-separated and locally of finite presentation over
S
), with an action of
\mathbb{G}_{m,S}
. We consider the functor of points:

\displaystyle X^{0}

\displaystyle:=Map^{\mathbb{G}_{m,S}}_{S}(S,X)

(3.5)

\displaystyle X^{\pm}

\displaystyle:=Map^{\mathbb{G}_{m,S}}_{S}((\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S})^{\pm},X)

where
(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S})^{+}
(resp.
(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S})^{-}
) is
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
with its canonical (resp. opposite)
\mathbb{G}_{m,S}
-action.
X^{0}
is the fixed points variety,
X^{+}
(resp.
X^{-}
) is called the attracting (resp. repelling) variety: informally, it classifies points
x\in X
with a limit
\lim_{t\to 0}t.x
(resp.
\lim_{t\to\infty}t.x
). Considering the commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S})^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{G}_{m,S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S})^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

One obtains from the mapping construction the hyperbolic localization diagram of
[
Bra02
]
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\iota}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\iota}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{-}}

Notice that
[X^{0}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
(resp.
[X^{\pm}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
) is a component of
{\rm Grad}([X/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
(resp.
{\rm Filt}([X/\mathbb{G}_{m}])
, hence they are representable quasi-separated algebraic spaces, locally of finite presentation over
S
, and
p^{\pm}
is of finite presentation. In particular, we see directly that our Theorem
3.1
is a direct generalization of the main result of Braden
[
Bra02
]
and Drinfeld-Gaitsgory
[
DG13
]
, which gives an adjunction
(p^{+})_{!}(\eta^{+})^{\ast}\rightleftharpoons(\eta^{-})_{!}(p^{-})^{\ast}
, where those functors are restricted to the triangulated subcategory of
\mathcal{D}(X)
generated by objects pulled back from
\mathcal{D}([X/\mathbb{G}_{m,S}])
.

Consider a smooth affine group
G
over
\mathbb{Z}
or a field, with a split maximal torus
T
. Consider
\mathcal{X}=[X/G_{S}]
with
X
an algebraic space (as always, assumed to be quasi-separated and locally of finite presentation over
S
), with an action of
G_{S}
over
S
. Consider:

(3.6)

\displaystyle\Lambda={\rm Hom}(\mathbb{G}_{m},T)

considered as a set of cocharacters of
G
(they represent class of cocharacters up to conjugation). For
\lambda\in\Lambda
, denote by
X^{0}_{\lambda},X^{+}_{\lambda}

the fixed locus and attracting locus of
X
with the induced action of
\mathbb{G}_{m,S}
. Considering the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
G
given by conjugation, denote by
L_{\lambda}:=G^{0}\subset G
the corresponding Levi subgroup, and by
P_{\lambda}:=G^{+}\subset G
the corresponding parabolic subgroup. Notice that
L_{\lambda,S}
(resp
P_{\lambda,S}
) acts naturally on
X^{0}_{\lambda}
(resp
X^{+}_{\lambda}
), and that there is a natural isomorphism
r_{\lambda}:X^{0}_{\lambda}\simeq X^{0}_{-\lambda}
.

Proposition 3.2
.

(Halpern-Leistner,
[
HL14
, Theo 1.4.7]
)
For
\mathcal{X}=[X/G_{S}]
with
X
an algebraic space, the
\Theta
-correspondence is given by:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigsqcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}{[X^{0}_{\lambda}/L_{\lambda,S}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigsqcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}{[X^{+}_{\lambda}/P_{\lambda,S}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[X/G_{S}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigsqcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\tilde{\iota}_{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigsqcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}r_{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigsqcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}p_{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigsqcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\eta_{\lambda}}

In some sense, in the smooth topology, one can always reduce to the case of schemes with
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action, thanks to the following result of Halpern-Leistner
[
Ayo07a
, Proposition 1.7.3]
:

Lemma 3.3
.

(Halpern-Leistner
[
HL14
, Lemma 4.4.6]
)
Consider a quasi-separated algebraic stack
\mathcal{X}
, locally of finite presentation, with affine stabilizers, over a quasi-separated and excellent algebraic space
S
. There is a smooth covering by affine schemes with
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action
f_{i}:[X_{i}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]\to\mathcal{X}
such that
{\rm Filt}(f_{i}):{\rm Filt}([X_{i}/\mathbb{G}_{m}])\to{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})
and
{\rm Grad}(f_{i}):{\rm Grad}([X_{i}/\mathbb{G}_{m}])\to{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})
are a smooth covering.

Remark that
[
HL14
, Lemma 4.4.6]
gives that, for a quasi-compact and quasi-separated stacks
\mathcal{X}
, there is a smooth covering
[X/(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n}]\to\mathcal{X}
for some
n
which is still surjective after passing to
{\rm Grad}
and
{\rm Filt}
. But, as we allow infinite covering, for
\mathcal{X}
quasi-separated, we can consider such covering for a Zariski covering by quasi-compact stacks, obtaining
f_{i}[X_{i}/(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n_{i}}]\to\mathcal{X}
which are surjective after passing to
{\rm Grad}
and
{\rm Filt}
, and then consider
f_{i,\lambda}:[X_{i}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]\to\mathcal{X}
for each cocharacter
\lambda
of
(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n_{i}}
, which are still surjective after passing to
{\rm Grad}
and
{\rm Filt}
.

3.2.
Proof of Braden-Drinfeld-Gaitsgory adjunction

3.2.1.
Construction of the unit

Consider the following diagram, with a Cartesian square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{\mathcal{X}}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\iota}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\iota}\circ r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r\circ p}

such that
p\circ\tilde{\iota}=r\circ p\circ\tilde{\iota}\circ r=Id
.

The main point is this analogue of
[
Dri13
, Proposition 1.6.2]
:

Lemma 3.4
.

The morphism
j:{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})\to{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{\mathcal{X}}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})
is an open and closed immersion.

Proof.

The morphism
j
is a section of the morphism:

(3.7)

\displaystyle\pi:{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{\mathcal{X}}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\overset{p_{1}}{\to}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\overset{p}{\to}{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})

it suffices to show that
\pi
is étale at the image of
j
. By construction,
\pi
is locally of finite type, hence it suffices to prove the formal lifting criterion. Consider an algebraic space
T\to S
, and a
T
-point of
{\rm Map}_{S}(B\mathbb{G}_{m,S},\mathcal{X})
given by
f:B\mathbb{G}_{m,T}\to\mathcal{X}
. Now, consider a square-zero extension
T^{\prime}
of
T
defined by a module
M
, and an extension
f^{\prime}:T^{\prime}\to\mathcal{X}
. We want to study the groupoid of extension of the commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbb{A}^{1}_{T}/\mathbb{G}_{m,T}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{G}_{m,T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbb{A}^{1}_{T}/\mathbb{G}_{m,T}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{G}_{m,T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

to a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbb{A}^{1}_{T^{\prime}}/\mathbb{G}_{m,T^{\prime}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{G}_{m,T^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbb{A}^{1}_{T^{\prime}}/\mathbb{G}_{m,T^{\prime}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Recall that objects
{\rm QCoh}(B\mathbb{G}_{m,T}))
are canonically identified with graded objects of
{\rm QCoh}(T)
. Extensions of
f
to
f^{\prime}
are classified by the groupoid:

(3.8)

\displaystyle{\rm Map}_{B\mathbb{G}_{m,T}}(f^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{X}},M)\simeq{\rm Map}_{T}((f^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{X}})^{0},M)

Extensions of
f
to
g
are classified by the groupoid:

(3.9)

\displaystyle{\rm Map}_{B\mathbb{G}_{m,T}}(f^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{X}},M[t])\simeq{\rm Map}_{T}((f^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{X}})^{\geq 0},M)

Extensions of
f
to
h
are classified by the groupoid:

(3.10)

\displaystyle{\rm Map}_{B\mathbb{G}_{m,T}}(f^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{X}},M[t^{-1}])\simeq{\rm Map}_{T}((f^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{X}})^{\leq 0},M)

and extensions of
f
to a map
T^{\prime}\to\mathcal{X}
are classified by the groupoid:

(3.11)

\displaystyle{\rm Map}_{T}(f^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{X}},M)

Finally, The groupoid of maps
T^{\prime}\to{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{\mathcal{X}}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})
extending
j\circ f:T\to
is given by the groupoid:

(3.12)

\displaystyle{\rm Map}_{T}((f^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{X}})^{\geq 0},M)\times_{{\rm Map}_{T}(f^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{X}},M)}{\rm Map}_{T}((f^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{X}})^{\leq 0},M)\simeq{\rm Map}_{T}((f^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{X}})^{0},M)

hence
\pi
is étale at
f:T\to\mathcal{X}
.
∎

The unit is then given by the formula inpired from
[
Bra02
]
:

(3.13)

\displaystyle Id\simeq(p\circ\tilde{\iota})_{!}(r\circ p\circ\tilde{\iota}\circ r)^{\ast}\simeq p_{!}(q_{1})_{!}j_{!}j^{\ast}(q_{2})^{\ast}(r\circ p)^{\ast}\to p_{!}(q_{1})_{!}(q_{2})^{\ast}(r\circ p)^{\ast}\simeq p_{!}\eta^{\ast}\eta_{!}(r\circ p)^{\ast}

where the arrow comes from the
(j_{!},j^{\ast})
adjunction (because
j
is open) and the last morphism is obtained by base change.

3.2.2.
Construction of the counit

The construction of the counit is the main input of Drinfeld-Gaitsgory
[
DG13
]
. We adapt their construction to the stack case. Consider the following commutative diagram with Cartesian squares:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r\circ p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

Consider
\mathbb{A}^{2}
with its antidiagonal
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action
s.(t,t^{\prime})\mapsto(st,s^{-1}t^{\prime})
. Consider the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-invariant map
\mathbb{A}^{2}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
given by
(t,t^{\prime})\mapsto tt^{\prime}
. It gives a flat GIT quotient
[\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}/\mathbb{G}_{m,S}]\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
, and
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
is an excellent quasi-separated algebraic space. Moreover, under our assumption,
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}
is quasi-separated, locally of finite presentation over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
, with affine stabilizers. Hence, from
[
AHR19
, Theorem 6.22]
, the mapping stack:

(3.14)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{X}}:={\rm Map}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}}([\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}/\mathbb{G}_{m,S}],\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}})

is an algebraic stack, quasi-separated and locally of finite presentation over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
, with affine stabilizers. Consider the commutative diagram with Cartesian square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{([\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}/\mathbb{G}_{m,S}])_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}/\mathbb{G}_{m,S}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

where
([\mathbb{A}^{2}/\mathbb{G}_{m}])_{0}
is the pushout:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathbb{G}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces([\mathbb{A}^{2}/\mathbb{G}_{m}])_{0}}

where the upper horizontal (resp left vertical) morphism is the morphism inducing
p
(resp
r\circ p
). Passing to the mapping stack, this gives commutative diagrams with Cartesian squares:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

Moreover, consider the commutative diagram with Cartesian squares of sections:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{([\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}/\mathbb{G}_{m,S}])_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}/\mathbb{G}_{m,S}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

where the two vertical central sections are given respectively by
\sigma_{1}:t\mapsto(1,t)
and
\sigma_{2}:t\mapsto(t,1)
. These two sections define two morphisms
\tilde{p}_{i}:\tilde{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}
over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
, whose restriction over
0
(resp
1
) are
\eta\circ q^{\prime}_{1}
and
\eta\circ q^{\prime}_{2}
(resp
Id
and
Id
), hence it gives a commutative and Cartesian diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}\times_{S}\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}\times_{S}\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}\times_{S}\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\circ q^{\prime}_{1}\times_{S}\eta\circ q^{\prime}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\mathcal{X}/S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

Notice now that, giving to
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
and
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}
its tautological
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action, and to
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}/\mathbb{G}_{m}
the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action induced by the diagonal
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}
;
[\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}/\mathbb{G}_{m,S}]\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
,
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathcal{X}
, and the two sections
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}\to[\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
have a natural
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant structure. Then
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}
has a natural
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action, and there is a natural
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant structure on
\tilde{p}:\tilde{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}\times_{S}\mathcal{X}}
. There is then a Cartesian diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}\times_{S}\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\tilde{\mathcal{X}}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}\times_{S}\mathcal{X}}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{q}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{p}}

In particular, by base change:

(3.15)

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{!}\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}\simeq\tilde{p}_{!}(\bar{q}^{\prime})^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}_{[\tilde{\mathcal{X}}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]}\simeq\bar{q}^{\ast}(\tilde{p}^{\prime})_{!}\mathbbm{1}_{[\tilde{\mathcal{X}}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]}

Hence, using Corollary
2.14
, we obtain a natural morphism:

(3.16)

\displaystyle(\eta\circ q^{\prime}_{1}\times_{S}\eta\circ q^{\prime}_{2})_{!}\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}\simeq 0^{\ast}\tilde{p}_{!}\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}\to 1^{\ast}\tilde{p}_{!}\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}\simeq(\Delta_{\mathcal{X}/S})_{!}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}

Using this morphism, the counit is given by the formula:

\displaystyle\eta_{!}(r\circ p)^{\ast}p_{!}\eta^{\ast}

\displaystyle\simeq(\eta\circ q^{\prime}_{2})_{!}(\eta\circ q^{\prime}_{1})^{\ast}

\displaystyle\simeq(pr_{2})_{!}((pr_{1}^{\ast}-\otimes_{\mathcal{X}\times_{S}\mathcal{X}}(\eta\circ q^{\prime}_{1}\times_{S}\eta\circ q^{\prime}_{2})_{!}\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})})

\displaystyle\to(pr_{2})_{!}((pr_{1}^{\ast}-\otimes_{\mathcal{X}\times_{S}\mathcal{X}}(\Delta_{\mathcal{X}/S})_{!}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}})

(3.17)

\displaystyle\simeq Id_{!}Id^{\ast}\simeq Id

where the first isomorphism is base change, and the isomorphisms of the second and last lines are obtained by the projection formula.

3.2.3.
Proof of the adjunction

We follow and adapt the arguments of
[
DG13
, Section 5]
. We obtain by composition two morphisms:

(3.18)

\displaystyle p_{!}\eta^{\ast}\to p_{!}\eta^{\ast}\circ\eta_{!}(r\circ p)^{\ast}\circ p_{!}\eta^{\ast}\to p_{!}\eta^{\ast}:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathcal{D}({\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X}))

(3.19)

\displaystyle\eta_{!}(r\circ p)^{\ast}\to\eta_{!}(r\circ p)^{\ast}\circ p_{!}\eta^{\ast}\circ\eta_{!}(r\circ p)^{\ast}\to\eta_{!}(r\circ p)^{\ast}:\mathcal{D}({\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X}))\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X})

and we have to show that these are the identity. We will show this for the first morphism. Consider the commutative and Cartesian diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{\mathcal{X}}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{\mathcal{X}}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{23}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{12}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r\circ p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

We consider the following diagram, obtained from the diagram of Section
3.2.1
by base change along
p:{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\to{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{\mathcal{X}}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{q}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{q}_{2}}

Using base change, the first arrow is identified with:

(3.20)

\displaystyle p_{!}\eta^{\ast}\simeq(\tilde{q}_{1})_{!}\tilde{j}_{!}\tilde{j}^{\ast}(\tilde{q}_{2})^{\ast}\to(\tilde{q}_{1})_{!}(\tilde{q}_{2})^{\ast}

obtained from the adjunction for the open immersion
\tilde{j}
. We consider the following diagram, obtained from the main diagram of Section
3.2.2
by base change along
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\eta}:\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{\mathcal{X}}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{X}}\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}\times_{S}{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}\times_{S}{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}\times_{S}{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{q}_{1}\times_{S}\tilde{q}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{p}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\times_{S}p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

where the arrow
\tilde{p}^{\prime}
is obtained by the composition:

(3.21)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{X}}\times_{\mathcal{X}}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}\times_{S}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}\overset{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{Id\times_{S}p}}{\to}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}\times_{S}{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}

Using base change, the second arrow is obtained from the morphism:

(3.22)

\displaystyle(\tilde{q}_{1}\times_{S}\tilde{q}_{2})_{!}\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{q}_{1}\times_{S}\tilde{q}_{2}}\simeq 0^{\ast}(\tilde{p}^{\prime})_{!}\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{\mathcal{X}}\times_{\mathcal{X}}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}\to 1^{\ast}(\tilde{p}^{\prime})_{!}\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{\mathcal{X}}\times_{\mathcal{X}}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}\simeq(\eta\times_{S}p)_{!}\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}

Lemma 3.5
.

There is a monomorphism of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
-stacks
\tilde{j}^{\prime}:\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}\to\tilde{\mathcal{X}}\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
such that the following diagram is commutative with Cartesian square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{\mathcal{X}}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{X}}\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}\times_{S}{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}\times_{S}{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}\times_{S}{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{j}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{q}_{1}\times_{S}\tilde{q}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{p}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\times_{S}p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

Proof.

Consider the commutative square of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
-stacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}\times_{S}[\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}/\mathbb{G}_{m,S}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}\times_{S}[\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}/\mathbb{G}_{m,S}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}/\mathbb{G}_{m,S}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}\times_{S}[\mathbb{G}_{m,S}/\mathbb{G}_{m,S}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,t)\mapsto(s,st)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,t)\mapsto(st,s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,t)\mapsto(s,t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,t)\mapsto(st,t^{-1})}

It defines a fpqc covering of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}\times_{S}[\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}/\mathbb{G}_{m,S}]
, which gives in particular a universal epimorphic family. Applying
{\rm Map}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}}(-,\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}})
, we obtain a commutative square of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
-stacks:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{p}_{2}}

which gives a morphism of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
-stacks:

(3.23)

\displaystyle\tilde{j}^{\prime}:\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}\to\tilde{\mathcal{X}}\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}

which is a monomorphism, from universal epimorphicity.

Over
1_{S}
, we obtain directly that
\tilde{j}^{\prime}_{1}
is the identity of
{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})
. Over
0_{S}
, the above diagram is the outer rectangle of the commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}/\mathbb{G}_{m,S}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathbb{G}_{m,S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}/\mathbb{G}_{m,S}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{([\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}/\mathbb{G}_{m}])_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S})^{-}/\mathbb{G}_{m,S}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbb{G}_{m,S}/\mathbb{G}_{m,S}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,t)\mapsto s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t\mapsto(0,t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t\mapsto t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t\mapsto t^{-1}}

where the left square is a pushout. It gives the commutative diagram diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\iota}\circ r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\iota}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

where the right square is Cartesian. The map
j:{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})\to{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{\mathcal{X}}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})
is induced by the right square, hence the map
j^{\prime}:{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\to{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{\mathcal{X}}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})
obtained by base change is induced by the outer rectangle, i.e. coincide with the restriction of
\tilde{j}^{\prime}_{0}
of
\tilde{j}^{\prime}
over
0_{S}
.
∎

As in
[
DG13
, Section 5]
, the above construction is the crucial step in the proof. In
[
Dri13
, Section 3]
, Drinfeld has that the analogue of
\tilde{j}^{\prime}
is an open immersion, which is a crucial point in their proof. We do not see how to generalize Drinfeld’s agument to our setting, fortunately we can use only the fact that
\tilde{j}^{\prime}
is a monomorphism, at the price of a subtle diagram chase.

Consider the following diagram of morphisms:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\eta\times_{S}p)_{!}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\eta\times_{S}p)_{!}0^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\eta\times_{S}p)_{!}1^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\eta\times_{S}p)_{!}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tilde{q}_{1}\times_{S}\tilde{q}_{2})_{!}(j^{\prime})_{!}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0^{\ast}(\tilde{p}^{\prime})_{!}(\tilde{j}^{\prime})_{!}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{\ast}(\tilde{p}^{\prime})_{!}(\tilde{j}^{\prime})_{!}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\eta\times_{S}p)_{!}(Id)_{!}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tilde{q}_{1}\times_{S}\tilde{q}_{2})_{!}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0^{\ast}(\tilde{p}^{\prime})_{!}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{\ast}(\tilde{p}^{\prime})_{!}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\eta\times_{S}p)_{!}\mathbbm{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where the left and right upper squares commutes by base change, and the upper central square commutes because the morphism of Corollary
2.14
commutes with specialization system, in particular with base change. Notice that the central horizontal arrow is then an isomorphism. As said above, under the equivalence
g_{!}f^{\ast}\simeq(p_{2})_{!}(p_{1}^{\ast}-\otimes(f\times g)_{!}\mathbbm{1})
, the morphism (
3.18
) is identified with the composition of the left vertical arrow with the lower horizontal arrow, and the upper horizontal and right vertical arrows are the identity, hence it suffices to prove that the lower rectangle is commutative. If we knew that
\tilde{j}^{\prime}
is an open immersion, one would to complete the lower rectangle, but we only know that
\tilde{j}^{\prime}
is a monomorphism. Recall that there is from the discussion of Section
2.2.3
a natural morphism
(\tilde{j}^{\prime})^{!}\to(\tilde{j}^{\prime})^{\ast}
, and that there is an adjunction morphism
(\tilde{j}^{\prime})_{!}(\tilde{j}^{\prime})^{!}\to Id
. The lower rectangle is identified with:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tilde{q}_{1}\times_{S}\tilde{q}_{2})_{!}(j^{\prime})_{!}(j^{\prime})^{\ast}0^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0^{\ast}(\tilde{p}^{\prime})_{!}(\tilde{j}^{\prime})_{!}(\tilde{j}^{\prime})^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{\ast}(\tilde{p}^{\prime})_{!}(\tilde{j}^{\prime})_{!}(\tilde{j}^{\prime})^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\eta\times_{S}p)_{!}(Id)_{!}(Id)^{\ast}1^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tilde{q}_{1}\times_{S}\tilde{q}_{2})_{!}(j^{\prime})_{!}(j^{\prime})^{!}0^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0^{\ast}(\tilde{p}^{\prime})_{!}(\tilde{j}^{\prime})_{!}(\tilde{j}^{\prime})^{!}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{\ast}(\tilde{p}^{\prime})_{!}(\tilde{j}^{\prime})_{!}(\tilde{j}^{\prime})^{!}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\eta\times_{S}p)_{!}(Id)_{!}(Id)^{!}1^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tilde{q}_{1}\times_{S}\tilde{q}_{2})_{!}0^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0^{\ast}(\tilde{p}^{\prime})_{!}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{\ast}(\tilde{p}^{\prime})_{!}\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\eta\times_{S}p)_{!}1^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where the central left horizontal arrow comes from the exchange transformation
Ex^{!\ast}
: it is an isomorphism because, from Lemma
2.13
,
0^{\ast}
commutes with specialization systems, in particular with
(j^{\prime})^{!}
. The upper left and right square commutes because the transformation
