(\tilde{j}^{\prime})^{!}\to(\tilde{j}^{\prime})^{\ast}
commutes with base change from Lemma
2.9
. The central squares trivially commutes, and the lower left and right square commutes from the compatibility of adjunction with base change. We obtain then that the upper rectangle and the lower rectangle commutes, hence the outer rectangle commutes, which finishes the proof that (
3.18
) is the identity.

To prove that (
3.19
) is the identity, the proof is formally similar. One consider instead the commutative diagram with Cartesian squares:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{\mathcal{X}}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{\mathcal{X}}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{23}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{12}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r\circ p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r\circ p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

and use the square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}\times_{S}[\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}/\mathbb{G}_{m,S}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}\times_{S}[\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}/\mathbb{G}_{m,S}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}/\mathbb{G}_{m,S}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}\times_{S}[\mathbb{G}_{m,S}/\mathbb{G}_{m,S}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,t)\mapsto(s,s^{-1}t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,t)\mapsto(st,t^{-1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,t)\mapsto(s,t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,t)\mapsto(t,st^{-1})}

to build an open immersion
\tilde{j}^{\prime\prime}:\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}\tilde{\mathcal{X}}
fitting in the commutative diagram with Cartesian squares:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{\mathcal{X}}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})\times_{S}\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})\times_{S}\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})\times_{S}\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{j}^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{q}_{1}\times_{S}\tilde{q}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{p}^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(r\circ p)\times_{S}\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

look at
[
DG13
, Section 5]
,
[
Dri13
, Section 3]
for more details.

3.3.
Functoriality of hyperbolic localization

3.3.1.
Functoriality with specialization systems

Lemma 3.6
.

If
\phi:\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{X}
is obtained by based change along a morphism of algebraic spaces
B^{\prime}\to B
, then
\eta_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}
,
p_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}
,
\tilde{\iota}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}
and
r_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}
are obtained by base change from
\eta_{\mathcal{X}}
,
p_{\mathcal{X}}
,
\tilde{\iota}_{\mathcal{X}}
and
r_{\mathcal{X}}
. This is also the case for all the functors build in the proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Proof.

All the morphisms in the proof of Theorem
3.1
are build using mapping spaces. Namely, for the quasi-separated algebraic space
T=S
(resp
T=\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
), one consider a morphism of
T
-stacks
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{Z}
having
T
as a good moduli space, and the morphism of mapping spaces
{\rm Map}_{T}(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{Y}_{T})\to Map(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}_{T})
. The morphism
{\rm Map}_{T}(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{T})\to{\rm Map}_{T}(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{X}_{T})\times_{{\rm Map}_{T}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime},\mathcal{X}_{T})}Ma_{T}p(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime},\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{T})
is obtained by base change from
{\rm Map}_{T}(\mathcal{Z},B^{\prime}_{T})\to{\rm Map}_{T}(\mathcal{Z},B_{T})\times_{{\rm Map}_{T}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime},B_{T})}{\rm Map}_{T}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime},B^{\prime}_{T})
, hence it suffices to show the result of the proposition of
B^{\prime}\to B
. But, from the universal property of good moduli spaces,
B^{(^{\prime})}_{T}\to{\rm Map}_{T}(\mathcal{Z}^{(^{\prime})},B^{(^{\prime})}_{T}))
is an isomorphism, hence this is trivial.
∎

Theorem 3.7
.

Given any specialization system
{\rm sp}:\mathcal{D}_{1}\to\mathcal{D}_{1}
over algebraic spaces
\eta\to B\leftarrow\sigma
, given any stack
f:\mathcal{X}\to B
, the natural morphism:

\displaystyle(p_{\sigma})_{!}(\eta_{\sigma})^{\ast}{\rm sp}_{f}\to{\rm sp}_{{\rm Grad}(f)}(p_{\eta})_{!}(\eta_{\eta})^{\ast}

(3.24)

\displaystyle(\eta_{\sigma})_{!}(p_{\sigma})^{\ast}{\rm sp}_{{\rm Grad}(f)}\to{\rm sp}_{f}(\eta_{\eta})_{!}(p_{\eta})^{\ast}

are isomorphisms.

Proof.

We begin to show the the exchange morphisms of the specialization systems are compatible with the adjunctions of Braden-Drinfeld-Gaitsgory theorem. We use for that the fact that all the diagrams used to define the unit and counit for
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
and
\mathcal{X}_{\eta}
are Cartesian over those used to define the unit and counit for
\mathcal{X}
from Lemma
3.6
, which allows to use the compatibility of specialization systems with base change. Consider the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}_{{\rm Grad}(f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{\sigma})_{!}(\eta_{\sigma})^{\ast}(\eta_{\sigma})_{!}((r\circ p)_{\sigma})^{\ast}{\rm sp}_{{\rm Grad}(f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{\sigma})_{!}(\eta_{\sigma})^{\ast}{\rm sp}_{f}(\eta_{\eta})_{!}((r\circ p)_{\eta})^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}_{{\rm Grad}(f)}(p_{\sigma})_{!}(\eta_{\sigma})^{\ast}(\eta_{\eta})_{!}((r\circ p)_{\eta})^{\ast}}

where the two vertical arrows come from the unit build in Section
3.2.1
. This construction use base change, which commutes with specialization systems, and the purity isomorphism for
j
, which commutes with specialization system from Lemma
2.10
, hence the above diagram is commutative. Consider the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\eta_{\sigma})_{!}((r\circ p)_{\sigma})^{\ast}(p_{\sigma})_{!}(\eta_{\sigma})^{\ast}{\rm sp}_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\eta_{\sigma})_{!}((r\circ p)_{\sigma})^{\ast}{\rm sp}_{{\rm Grad}(f)}(p_{\eta})_{!}(\eta_{\eta})^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}_{f}(\eta_{\sigma})_{!}((r\circ p)_{\sigma})^{\ast}(p_{\eta})_{!}(\eta_{\eta})^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}_{f}}

where the two vertical arrows come from the counit build in Section
3.2.2
. Notice that, denoting by
\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2}:\tilde{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}
the two projections of
\tilde{p}
, this morphism can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\eta_{!}(r\circ p)^{\ast}p_{!}\eta^{\ast}

\displaystyle\simeq(\eta\circ q^{\prime}_{2})_{!}(\eta\circ q^{\prime}_{1})^{\ast}

\displaystyle\simeq 0^{\ast}(\tilde{p}_{2})_{!}(\tilde{p}_{1})^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}

\displaystyle\to 1^{\ast}(\tilde{p}_{2})_{!}(\tilde{p}_{1})^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}

(3.25)

\displaystyle\simeq Id_{!}Id^{\ast}\simeq Id

the second morphism commutes with specialization systems from Corollary
2.14
, and the other isomorphisms commutes with specialization systems as they are obtained by functoriality and base change, hence the above diagram commutes. The end of the proof is a formal game of adjunction. We have the commutative diagram (where we have removed the subscripts for readability):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{!}\eta^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}p_{!}\eta^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{!}\eta^{\ast}\eta_{!}(r\circ p)^{\ast}p_{!}\eta^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{!}\eta^{\ast}\eta_{!}(r\circ p)^{\ast}{\rm sp}p_{!}\eta^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{!}\eta^{\ast}{\rm sp}\eta_{!}(r\circ p)^{\ast}p_{!}\eta^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}p_{!}\eta^{\ast}\eta_{!}(r\circ p)^{\ast}p_{!}\eta^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{!}\eta^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}p_{!}\eta^{\ast}}

by the adjunction property, the two outer diagonal paths are the identity, hence the morphism
{\rm sp}p_{!}\eta^{\ast}\to p_{!}\eta^{\ast}{\rm sp}
obtained by the staircase central composition is the inverse of
p_{!}\eta^{\ast}{\rm sp}\to{\rm sp}p_{!}\eta^{\ast}
. A dual reasoning gives an inverse of
\eta_{!}(r\circ p)^{\ast}{\rm sp}\to{\rm sp}\eta_{!}(r\circ p)^{\ast}
, but
r
is an involution, hence
r^{\ast}
trivially commutes with specialization systems, then
\eta_{!}p^{\ast}{\rm sp}\to{\rm sp}\eta_{!}p^{\ast}
is an isomorphism.
∎

In particular, this implies the commutation of hyperbolic localization with
g^{\ast},g^{!},g_{!},g_{\ast}
, when
g
is obtained by base change from a morphism of algebraic spaces, as obtained in
[
Ric16
, Proposition 3.1]
, and with (monodromic) nearby and vanishing cycles, as in
[
Ric16
, Theorem 3.3]
. We put also this simple result for reference:

Lemma 3.8
.

Hyperbolic localization commutes with exterior product, namely, given
\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{2}
, one has a natural isomorphisms:

(3.26)

\displaystyle(p_{\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}})^{\ast}(-\boxtimes_{S}-)\simeq((p_{\mathcal{X}_{1}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{X}_{1}})^{!})\boxtimes_{S}((p_{\mathcal{X}_{2}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{X}_{2}})^{!})

satisfying associativity and commutativity

Proof.

From the monoidality of the mapping stack construction,
p_{\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}}=p_{\mathcal{X}_{1}}\times_{S}p_{\mathcal{X}_{2}}
and
\eta_{\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}}=\eta_{\mathcal{X}_{1}}\times_{S}\eta_{\mathcal{X}_{2}}
, hence this follows from the monoidality of the four functors.
∎

3.3.2.
Functoriality with smooth morphisms

We want to talk about the behavior cotangent complex of stacks, or morphisms of stacks, under the construction
{\rm Grad}(-)
and
{\rm Filt}(-)
. Those behaves better in the world of derived stacks, but at the end we will be interested in cotangent complexes of smooth morphisms, such that these will agree with their classical truncations. We follow here the presentation in
[
HL14
, Section 1.2]
and
[
HL20
, Section 1]
, adapting it to our non-derived setting.

For a stack with affine stabilizers
\mathcal{X}
,
{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})
has a natural weak action action of the monoid
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
, which gives from
[
HL20
, Lemma 1.5.3]
(when we consider them as derived stacks) a natural
\mathbb{Z}
-grading on any quasi-coherent complex
\mathcal{E}=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{E}^{n}
. Similarly,
{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})
has a natural weak action of the monoid
\Theta
, such that, from
[
HL20
, Proposition 1.1.2]
(when we consider them as derived stacks), there is a baric structure on
{\rm QCoh}({\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X}))
. It gives roughly a version of truncation at the level of derived category, in particular, for
w\in\mathbb{Z}
, each object lies in an exact triangle:

(3.27)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\geq w}\to\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}^{<w}

We use freely the notation
\mathcal{E}^{I},\mathcal{F}^{I}
for any interval
I
, defined in the obvious way. From
[
HL20
, Lemma 1.1.5, Lemma 1.5.3]
, these structures are compatible in the sense that:

\displaystyle\tilde{\iota}^{\ast}(\mathcal{F}^{\geq w})=\bigoplus_{n\geq w}(\tilde{\iota}^{\ast}\mathcal{F})^{n}\quad

\displaystyle\tilde{\iota}^{\ast}(\mathcal{F}^{<w})=\bigoplus_{n<w}(\tilde{\iota}^{\ast}\mathcal{F})^{n}

(3.28)

\displaystyle(p^{\ast}\mathcal{E})^{\geq w}=\bigoplus_{n\geq w}p^{\ast}(\mathcal{E}^{n})\quad

\displaystyle(p^{\ast}\mathcal{E})^{<w}=\bigoplus_{n<w}p^{\ast}(\mathcal{E}^{n})

In particular, when
\mathcal{F}
is a locally free sheaf,
(p\circ\tilde{i})^{\ast}\mathcal{F}
is the graded objects associated to the filtration. This is a transcription for non-derived stacks of the results
[
HL20
, Lemma 1.3.2, Lemma 1.5.5]
:

Lemma 3.9
.

(Halpern-Leistner,
[
HL20
]
)
For
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
a smooth morphism of stacks,
{\rm Grad}(\phi)
and
{\rm Filt}(\phi)
are smooth too, and one has canonical isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{{\rm Filt}(\phi)}\simeq\eta^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{X}})^{\leq 0}

(3.29)

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{{\rm Grad}(\phi)}\simeq\iota^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{X}})^{0}

Proof.

As explained in
[
HL20
, Section 1.2]
, there is a derived version of mapping stack for derived stacks (as functors of groupoids on the
(\infty,1)
categories of connective simplicial rings), such that, extending trivially
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
and
\Theta
, one obtains derived versions
\underline{{\rm Grad}}(-)
and
\underline{{\rm Filt}}(-)
of the
{\rm Grad}(-)
and
{\rm Filt}(-)
functors. Denoting by
\underline{\mathcal{X}}
the trivial derived extension of a classical stack, we have:

\displaystyle\underline{{\rm Grad}}(\underline{\mathcal{X}})^{cl}={\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})

(3.30)

\displaystyle\underline{{\rm Filt}}(\underline{\mathcal{X}})^{cl}={\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})

and similarly for morphisms (where the superscript
cl
denotes the classical truncation). By
[
HL20
, Lemma 1.3.2, Lemma 1.5.5]
, one has canonical identifications:

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{\underline{{\rm Filt}}(\underline{\mathcal{X}})}\simeq\underline{\eta}^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{\underline{\mathcal{X}}})^{\leq 0}

(3.31)

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{\underline{{\rm Grad}}(\underline{\mathcal{X}})}\simeq\underline{\iota}^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{\underline{\mathcal{X}}})^{0}

Now, consider a morphism
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
. Using the exact triangle of cotangent complexes for
\underline{\phi}
, and applying
\underline{\eta}^{\ast}
to those of
\underline{{\rm Filt}}(\underline{\phi})
(resp.
\underline{\iota}^{\ast}
to those of
\underline{{\rm Grad}}(\underline{\phi})
), using the fact that the truncations commutes with pullbacks, we obtain canonical isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{\underline{{\rm Filt}}(\underline{\phi})}\simeq\underline{\eta}^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{\underline{\phi}})^{\leq 0}

(3.32)

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{\underline{{\rm Grad}}(\underline{\phi})}\simeq\underline{\iota}^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{\underline{\phi}})^{0}

(this was noticed in the proof of
[
HL20
, Corollary 1.3.2.1]
for
\underline{{\rm Filt}}(\underline{\phi}
). In particular, if
\phi
is smooth, hence
\mathbb{L}_{\underline{\phi}}
is perfect in amplitude
[-1,0]
, because pullbacks and truncation functors are exact,
\mathbb{L}_{\underline{{\rm Filt}}(\underline{\phi})}
and
\mathbb{L}_{\underline{{\rm Grad}}(\underline{\phi})}
are also perfect in amplitude
[-1,0]
. One obtains from
[
HL14
, Lemma 1.2.4]
that their pullbacks to the classical truncations
{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})
and
{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})
agree with
\mathbb{L}_{{\rm Grad}(\phi)}
and
\mathbb{L}_{{\rm Filt}(\phi)}
. Using the commutations of the truncation functors with pullbacks, one obtains the isomorphisms of the proposition from the derived version above. In particular,
{\rm Grad}(\phi)
and
{\rm Filt}(\phi)
are smooth.
∎

The following is a relative and stacky version of the main result of Bialynicki-Birula
[
BB73
]
:

Proposition 3.10
.

Consider a smooth morphism of stacks
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
. Then
\pi:{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\to{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{Y})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{Y})
is canonically an affine bundle stack modeled on
(pr_{1})^{\ast}((\iota^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\phi})^{<0}))
.

Proof.

We will prove that at the level of the functors of groupoids, using deformation theory. The groupoid of
{\rm Spec}(A)
-point of
{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{Y})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{Y})
is the groupoid of commutative diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times{\rm Spec}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Spec}(A[t]/t^{n})/\mathbb{G}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Spec}(A[t]/t^{n+1})/\mathbb{G}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Spec}(A[t])/\mathbb{G}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{f}}

consider the point
g:{\rm Spec}(A)\to{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{Y})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{Y})
corresponding to this diagram. In particular,
p_{2}\circ g:{\rm Spec}(A)\to\mathcal{X}
is the
{\rm Spec}(A)
-point defined by
f
. We want to study the deformation-theoretic problem of lifting
f_{n}
to
f_{n+1}
, for
n\geq 1
. The square-zero ideal
I
of the closed immersion
{\rm Spec}(A[t]/t^{n})/\mathbb{G}_{m}\to{\rm Spec}(A[t]/t^{n+1})/\mathbb{G}_{m}
is the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant ideal
(t^{n})/(t^{n+1})
, which is, as a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant
A[t]/t^{n}
-module, identified with
(i_{n})_{\ast}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\langle n\rangle\boxtimes A)
. We have then:

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}{\rm Hom}_{{\rm Spec}(A[t]/t^{n})/\mathbb{G}_{m}}((f_{n})^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\phi},I)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{R}{\rm Hom}_{{\rm Spec}(A[t]/t^{n})/\mathbb{G}_{m}}((f_{n})^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\phi},(i_{n})_{\ast}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\langle n\rangle\boxtimes A))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbb{R}{\rm Hom}_{{\rm Spec}(A)\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}}(f^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\phi},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\langle n\rangle\boxtimes A)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbb{R}{\rm Hom}_{{\rm Spec}(A)}((f^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\phi})^{-n},A)

(3.33)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbb{R}{\rm Hom}_{{\rm Spec}(A)}(g^{\ast}(pr_{1})^{\ast}(\iota^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\phi})^{-n},A)

here the second line holds by adjunction, the third by the definition of the truncation functor given below
[
HL20
, Definition 1.1.1]
, and the last line from the definition of the truncation functor on
{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})
. Given a lift
f_{n}
, according to
[
Pri13
, Theorem 8.5]
, the obstruction to extends
f_{n}
to
f_{n+1}
lies in:

(3.34)

\displaystyle{\rm Ext}^{1}_{{\rm Spec}(A[t]/t^{n})/\mathbb{G}_{m}}((f_{n})^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\phi},I)\simeq{\rm Ext}^{1}_{{\rm Spec}(A)}(g^{\ast}(pr_{1})^{\ast}(\iota^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\phi})^{-n},A)

which vanishes because the right hand term is perfect in degree
[0,1]
. Then still according to
[
Pri13
, Theorem 8.5]
, the groupoid of extension is naturally a torsor over:

(3.35)

\displaystyle{\rm Map}_{{\rm Spec}(A[t]/t^{n})/\mathbb{G}_{m}}((f_{n})^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\phi},I)\simeq{\rm Map}_{{\rm Spec}(A)}(g^{\ast}(pr_{1})^{\ast}(\iota^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\phi})^{-n},A)

Using the coherent completeness of
\Theta_{{\rm Spec}(A)}
along
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times{\rm Spec}(A)

[
AHR20
, Proposition 5.18]
and the Tannaka duality result
[
AHR20
, Corollary 2.8]
, the groupoid of lifts
\bar{f}
is equivalent to the groupoid of coherent systems of lifts
f_{n}
. Hence, the groupoid of lifts
\bar{f}
, i.e. of
{\rm Spec}(A)
-points of
{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})
over
g:{\rm Spec}(A)\to{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{Y})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{Y})
, is naturally a torsor over the Abelian groupoid:

(3.36)

\displaystyle{\rm Map}_{{\rm Spec}(A)}(g^{\ast}(pr_{1})^{\ast}(\iota^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\phi})^{<0},A)

which is by definition the groupoid of
{\rm Spec}(A)
-points of
\mathbb{V}_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{Y})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{Y})}((pr_{1})^{\ast}(\iota^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\phi})^{<0})
. This action is obviously functorial in
A
, hence we have built a structure of
\mathbb{V}_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{Y})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{Y})}((pr_{1})^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{\phi}^{<0}))
-torsor on
{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\to{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{Y})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{Y})
.
∎

Remark 3.11
.

In particular, if
\mathcal{X}
is smooth over
\mathcal{Y}=S
, from Lemma
3.6
,
{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(S)}{\rm Filt}(S)\simeq{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})
. One obtains that
{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\to{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})
is an affine bundle stack over
(\iota^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{X}})^{<0}
. In this case,
\tilde{\iota}:{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})\to{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})
gives a canonical section, hence
{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\to{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})
is identified with the vector bundle stack
\mathbb{V}_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}((\iota^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{X}})^{<0})
. In particular, if
\mathcal{X}=[X/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
,
[X^{+}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]\to[X^{0}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
is a component of
{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\to{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})
, and, passing to the cover
X^{0}\to[X^{0}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
,
one find the classical result of Bialynicki-Birula
[
BB73
]
, saying that
X^{+}\to X^{0}
is identified with the vector bundle
\mathbb{V}_{X}((\mathbb{L}_{X}|_{X^{0}})^{<0})
. In this sense, our result is a relative and stacky version of
[
BB73
]
.

From now on, we will slightly abuse the notations by denoting
\mathbb{L}_{\phi}^{I}
for
(\iota^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\phi})^{I}
.

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
be a smooth morphism of stacks, and suppose that the coefficient system satisfies étale descent, or that
\pi:{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\to{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{Y})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{Y})
has Nisnevich-local sections (it is plausible that this condition is always satisfied, see Remark
2.6
). There is a natural isomorphism:

(3.37)

\displaystyle(p_{\mathcal{X}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{X}})^{\ast}\phi^{\ast}\simeq\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{\phi}^{<0}}{\rm Grad}(\phi)^{\ast}(p_{\mathcal{Y}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{Y}})^{\ast}

It is compatible with composition, namely for
\phi^{\prime}:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{Z}
smooth, the following square of isomorphisms is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{\mathcal{X}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{X}})^{\ast}\phi^{\ast}(\phi^{\prime})^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{\phi}^{<0}}{\rm Grad}(\phi)^{\ast}(p_{\mathcal{Y}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{Y}})^{\ast}(\phi^{\prime})^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{\phi}^{<0}}{\rm Grad}(\phi)^{\ast}\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{\phi^{\prime}}^{<0}}{\rm Grad}(\phi^{\prime})^{\ast}(p_{\mathcal{Z}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{Z}})^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{\mathcal{X}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{X}})^{\ast}(\phi^{\prime}\circ\phi)^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{\phi^{\prime}\circ\phi}^{<0}}(p_{\mathcal{Z}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{Z}})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where the right vertical arrow comes from the exact triangle
{\rm Grad}(\phi)^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{\phi^{\prime}}^{<0})\to\mathbb{L}_{\phi^{\prime}\circ\phi}^{<0}\to\mathbb{L}_{\phi}^{<0}
. It is also compatible with exterior tensor product, using the isomorphism of Lemma
3.8
.

Proof.

We consider the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{Y})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathcal{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathcal{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathcal{Y}}}

We consider first the isomorphism:

(3.38)

\displaystyle(\eta_{\mathcal{X}})^{\ast}\phi^{\ast}\simeq{\rm Filt}(\phi)^{\ast}(\eta_{\mathcal{Y}})^{\ast}

Proposition
3.10
gives that
\pi
is an affine bundle stack, and Lemma
2.4
gives that it is smooth of cotangent bundle
\pi^{\ast}(pr_{1})^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{\phi}^{<0})
. Using Lemma
2.5
,
\pi_{!}\pi^{!}\to Id
is an isomorphisms. We obtain then:

(3.39)

\displaystyle\pi_{!}\pi^{\ast}\simeq\pi_{!}\Sigma^{-\pi^{\ast}(pr_{1})^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{\phi}^{<0})}\pi^{!}\simeq\Sigma^{-(pr_{1})^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{\phi}^{<0}))}\pi_{!}\pi^{!}\simeq\Sigma^{-(pr_{1})^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{\phi}^{<0})}

where the first isomorphism is the purity isomorphism. With that, we obtain a ’base change isomorphisms’:

(3.40)

\displaystyle(p_{\mathcal{X}})_{!}{\rm Filt}(\phi)^{\ast}\simeq(pr_{1})_{!}\pi_{!}\pi^{\ast}(pr_{2})^{\ast}\simeq(pr_{1})_{!}\Sigma^{-(pr_{1})^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{\phi}^{<0})}(pr_{2})^{\ast}\simeq\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{\phi}^{<0}}(pr_{1})_{!}(pr_{2})^{\ast}\simeq\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{\phi}^{<0}}{\rm Grad}(\phi)^{\ast}(p_{\mathcal{Y}})_{!}

which gives the claimed isomorphism.

The isomorphism (
3.38
) is obviously compatible with composition, so we want to show that the isomorphism (
3.40
) is compatible with composition. We have the following commutative diagram with Cartesian square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{Z})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{Y})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{Y})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{Z})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{Y})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\phi^{\prime}\circ\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\phi)\times Id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\pi}_{\phi^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\phi^{\prime}}}

From the compatibility of the counit with composition, and the compatibility of the purity isomorphism with composition (Lemma
2.11
), one has a commutative square of isomorphisms:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tilde{\pi}_{\phi^{\prime}})_{!}(\pi_{\phi})_{!}(\pi_{\phi})^{\ast}(\tilde{\pi}_{\phi^{\prime}})^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tilde{\pi}_{\phi^{\prime}})_{!}\Sigma^{-(pr_{1})^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{\phi}^{<0})}(\tilde{\pi}_{\phi^{\prime}})^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-(pr_{1})^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{\phi}^{<0})}\Sigma^{-({\rm Grad}(\phi)\times Id)^{\ast}(pr_{1})^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{\phi^{\prime}}^{<0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{\phi^{\prime}\circ\phi})_{!}(\pi_{\phi^{\prime}\circ\phi})^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-(pr_{1})^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{\phi^{\prime}\circ\phi}^{<0})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

from the compatibility of the counit with base change, and the compatibility of the purity isomorphism with base change (Lemma
2.10
), the upper right vertical arrow is obtained by base change from
(\pi_{\phi^{\prime}})_{!}(\pi_{\phi^{\prime}})^{\ast}\simeq\Sigma^{-(pr_{1})^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{\phi^{\prime}}^{<0})}
. It follows then that (
3.40
) is compatible with composition.

The statement about products follows directly, as all our constructions are monoidal.
∎

Remark 3.13
.

In particular, when
\mathcal{X}\to S
is smooth (in this case,
\pi=p
has always a section
\tilde{\iota}
), one obtains the ” Bialynicki-Birula decomposition”:

(3.41)

\displaystyle p_{!}\eta^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\simeq\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{X}}^{<0}}\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}

and, if
\mathcal{X}=[X/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
:

(3.42)

\displaystyle(p^{+})_{!}(\eta^{+})^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}_{X}\simeq\Sigma^{-(\mathbb{L}_{X}|_{X^{0}})^{<0}}\mathbbm{1}_{X^{0}}

Lemma 3.14
.

Consider specialization system
{\rm sp}:\mathcal{D}_{1}\to\mathcal{D}_{1}
over algebraic spaces
\eta\to B\leftarrow\sigma
. Consider a smooth morphism of stacks
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
. Then the following square of isomorphisms is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{\mathcal{X}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{X}})^{\ast}\phi^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}(p_{\mathcal{X}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{X}})^{\ast}\phi^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{\phi}^{<0}}{\rm Grad}(\phi)^{\ast}(p_{\mathcal{Y}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{Y}})^{\ast}{\rm sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sp}\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{\phi}^{<0}}{\rm Grad}(\phi)^{\ast}(p_{\mathcal{Y}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{Y}})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Proof.

Notice that, from Lemma
3.6
, all the diagram considered over
\eta
and
\sigma
are obtain by base change from those considered over
B
. The morphism (
3.38
) is obviously compatible with specialization systems. Specialization systems are compatible with counit, and with the purity isomorphism (Lemma
2.10
), hence the isomorphism (
3.39
) commutes with specialization systems. Using compatibility of specialization systems with base change, the isomorphism (
3.40
) commutes then also with specialization systems, hence we are done.
∎

3.3.3.
Functoriality with closed pullbacks

This the following (iso)morphism will be useful to restrict vanishing cycles to the critical locus:

Lemma 3.15
.

Given a closed immersion
i:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{X}
, there is a natural morphism:

(3.43)

\displaystyle{\rm Grad}(i)^{\ast}(p_{\mathcal{X}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{X}})^{\ast}\to(p_{\mathcal{Z}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{Z}})^{\ast}i^{\ast}

compatible with exterior products, composition of closed immersions, and with the isomorphism of Proposition
3.12
under base change. It is an isomorphism when applied on objects of
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X})
supported on
\mathcal{Z}
.

Proof.

Consider the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{Z})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathcal{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathcal{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathcal{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathcal{U}}}

By
[
HL14
, Corollary 1.1.7, Proposition 1.3.1 2)]
, because
i
is closed we have
{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{R})={\rm Grad}(\mathcal{U})\times_{\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{R}
and
{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{R})={\rm Filt}(\mathcal{U})\times_{\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{R}
. In particular the right square diagram is Cartesian,
{\rm Filt}(i)=pr_{2}\circ\hat{i}
and
pr_{2}
are closed immersions, hence
\hat{i}
is a closed immersion. We consider then the following sequence of (iso)morphisms:

(3.44)

\displaystyle{\rm Grad}(i)^{\ast}(p_{\mathcal{X}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{X}})^{\ast}\simeq(pr_{1})_{!}(pr_{2})^{\ast}(\eta_{\mathcal{X}})^{\ast}\to(pr_{1})_{!}\hat{i}_{!}\hat{i}^{\ast}(pr_{2})^{\ast}(\eta_{\mathcal{X}})^{\ast}\simeq(p_{\mathcal{Z}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{Z}})^{\ast}i^{\ast}

where the second morphism comes from
Id\to\hat{i}_{!}\hat{i}^{\ast}
, as
\hat{i}
is a closed immersion. If an object
F\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X})
is supported on
\mathcal{Z}
, by base change
(\eta_{\mathcal{X}})^{\ast}F
is supported on
{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{Z})
, hence this morphism is an isomorphism.

If we consider a second closed immersion
i^{\prime}:\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{Z}
, we want to show that the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(i^{\prime})^{\ast}{\rm Grad}(i)^{\ast}(p_{\mathcal{X}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{X}})^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(i^{\prime})^{\ast}(p_{\mathcal{Z}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{Z}})^{\ast}i^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}})^{\ast}(i^{\prime})^{\ast}i^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(i\circ i^{\prime})^{\ast}(p_{\mathcal{X}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{X}})^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}})^{\ast}(i\circ i^{\prime})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

The proof is similar to the proof of the similar statement in Proposition
3.12
, and we have to prove that
Id\to\hat{i}_{!}\hat{i}^{\ast}
is compatible with composition of closed immersions and base change. From Lemma
2.9
and
2.11
,
\hat{i}_{!}\simeq\hat{i}_{\ast}
is compatible with base change and composition, and
Id\to\hat{i}_{\ast}\hat{i}^{\ast}
is also by properties of the adjunctions, hence
Id\to\hat{i}_{!}\hat{i}^{\ast}
is compatible with base change and composition.

Consider now
\phi:\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{X}
smooth,
i:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{X}
a closed immersion, and
\phi^{\prime}:\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{Z}
,
i^{\prime}:\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{X}
obtained by base change. We want to show that the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{\phi^{\prime}}^{<0}}{\rm Grad}(\phi^{\prime})^{\ast}{\rm Grad}(i)^{\ast}(p_{\mathcal{X}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{X}})^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{\phi^{\prime}}^{<0}}{\rm Grad}(\phi^{\prime})^{\ast}(p_{\mathcal{Z}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{Z}})^{\ast}i^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}})^{\ast}(\phi^{\prime})^{\ast}i^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(i^{\prime})^{\ast}\Sigma^{-\mathbb{L}_{\phi}^{<0}}{\rm Grad}(\phi)^{\ast}(p_{\mathcal{X}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{X}})^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(i^{\prime})^{\ast}(p_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}})^{\ast}\phi^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}})_{!}(\eta_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}})^{\ast}(i^{\prime})^{\ast}\phi^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

As said above, the morphism
Id\to\hat{i}_{!}\hat{i}^{\ast}
and
(\pi_{\phi})_{!}(\pi_{\phi})^{\ast}\simeq\Sigma^{-(pr_{1})^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{\phi}^{<0})}
are compatible with base change. From
2.9
, the isomorphism of specialization systems
(\pi_{\phi})_{!}(\pi_{\phi})^{\ast}\simeq\Sigma^{-(pr_{1})^{\ast}(\mathbb{L}_{\phi}^{<0})}
commutes with
\hat{i}_{!}\simeq\hat{i}_{\ast}
, hence also with
Id\to\hat{i}_{!}\hat{i}^{\ast}
. We obtain then the desired result by base change in the following commutative diagram with Cartesian squares:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{Z})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{Z})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X}^{\prime})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X}^{\prime})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{i}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\hat{i}}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\phi^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{i}_{i^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(\phi^{\prime})\times Id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Grad}(i^{\prime})\times Id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\pi}_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\phi}}

The statement about products follows directly, as all our constructions are monoidal.
∎

3.3.4.
Functoriality with
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-bundles

Consider a principal
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-bundle
\pi:P\to\mathcal{X}
(i.e. , a finite map of degree
2
). Consider the locally constant object:

(3.45)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{P}:={\rm cofib}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\to\pi_{\ast}\pi^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}})\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X})

Following the notation of
[
BBD+15
, Definition 2.9, remark 2.20]
, we denote:

(3.46)

\displaystyle-\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}P:={\rm cofib}(Id\to\pi_{\ast}\pi^{\ast})\simeq-\otimes\mathcal{L}_{P}:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X})

As
\pi
is étale and proper, by smooth and proper base change, this defines a morphism of coefficient systems
\mathcal{D}|_{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathcal{D}|_{\mathcal{X}}
, i.e. there are natural isomorphisms of commutation with the six functors.

Given
\pi_{i}:P_{i}\to\mathcal{X}
(resp.
\pi_{i}:P_{i}\to\mathcal{X}_{i}
),
i=1,2
, we denote by
P_{1}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}P_{2}
(resp.
P_{1}\boxtimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}P_{2}
) the (resp. exterior) product of
P,P^{\prime}
as
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-bundles.

Lemma 3.16
.

The operation
P\to-\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}P
enhance to a symmetric monoidal functor from
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-bundles over
\mathcal{X}
to involutive isomorphisms of coefficient systems
\mathcal{D}|_{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathcal{D}|_{\mathcal{X}}
. It is compatible with exterior tensor product, namely for
\pi_{i}:P_{i}\to\mathcal{X}_{i}
for
i=1,2
, there is a natural isomorphism of involution of coefficient systems:

(3.47)

\displaystyle(-\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}P_{1})\boxtimes(-\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}P_{2})\simeq(-\boxtimes-)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}(P_{1}\boxtimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}P_{2})

which is symmetric monoidal.

Proof.

For the trivial
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-bundle
P_{triv}=\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
, one has the exact triangle:

(3.48)

\displaystyle Id\overset{(Id,Id)}{\to}\pi_{\ast}\pi^{\ast}=Id\oplus Id\overset{pr_{1}}{\to}Id

where one projects to the component corresponding to
1\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
, which gives a canonical identification
-\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}P_{triv}\simeq Id
. For
P,P^{\prime}
two
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-bundles, consider the sequence of
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-principal bundles:

(3.49)

\displaystyle\tilde{\pi}:P\times_{\mathcal{X}}P^{\prime}\to P\times_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}P^{\prime}\to\mathcal{X}

where the first map is the trivial
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-bundle. Now, we have:

\displaystyle-\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}P\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}P^{\prime}

\displaystyle:={\rm cofib}(Id\to(\pi^{\prime})_{\ast}(\pi^{\prime})^{\ast})\circ{\rm cofib}(Id\to\pi_{\ast}\pi^{\ast})

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm cofib}(Id\to(\pi^{\prime})_{\ast}(\pi^{\prime})^{\ast}\pi_{\ast}\pi^{\ast})

(3.50)

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm cofib}(Id\to\tilde{\pi}_{\ast}\tilde{\pi}^{\ast})

where the third line follows from base change (recalling that
\pi
is étale and proper). Now, notice that
P\times_{\mathcal{X}}P^{\prime}=(P\times_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}P^{\prime})\times_{\mathcal{X}}P_{triv}
, hence we have a canonical isomorphism:

(3.51)

\displaystyle-\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}P\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}P^{\prime}\simeq-\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}(P\times_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}P^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}P_{triv}\simeq-\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}(P\times_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}P^{\prime})

which is symmetric monoidal, and commutes with the six operations by smooth and proper base change. The isomorphism of commutation with the exterior tensor product can be defined formally from the isomorphism of commutation with tensor product and pullbacks.
∎

Lemma 3.17
.

Consider a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-principal bundle
P\to\mathcal{X}
:
{\rm Grad}(P)\to\mathcal{X}
and
{\rm Filt}(P)\to\mathcal{X}
are
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-principal bundles, and there is a canonical isomorphism:

(3.52)

\displaystyle p_{!}\eta^{\ast}(-\boxtimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}P)\simeq p_{!}\eta^{\ast}\boxtimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}{\rm Grad}(P)

commuting with the isomorphisms of commutation with exterior tensor product of Lemma
3.8
and the isomorphism of commutation with smooth pullbacks of Proposition
3.12
.

Proof.

As
P\to\mathcal{X}
is étale and representable, we have
{\rm Grad}(P)\simeq{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})\times_{\mathcal{X}}P
by
[
HL14
, Corollary 1.1.7]
and
{\rm Filt}(P)\simeq{\rm Filt}(\mathcal{X})\times_{{\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X})}{\rm Grad}(P)
by Proposition
3.10
. Then
{\rm Grad}(P)\to\mathcal{X}
and
{\rm Filt}(P)\to\mathcal{X}
are
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-principal bundles obtained from
P\to\mathcal{X}
by base change. By Lemma
3.16
,
-\boxtimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}P
enhance to an involution of coefficient systems over
\mathcal{X}
. We can applies this involution to the functor
p_{!}\eta^{\ast}
, which gives the claimed isomorphism, which is compatible with exterior tensor product from Lemma
3.16
. Applying the involution of coefficient systems
-\boxtimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}P
to the isomorphism of Proposition
3.12
, we obtained the claimed compatibility.
∎

4.
Monodromic mixed Hodge modules and perverse Nori motives

4.1.
Six functor formalisms for mixed Hodge modules and Perverse Nori motives

In this section, our base will be an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic
0
, and all stacks will be algebraic
1
-stacks locally of finite type over
k
(in particular, they are quasi-separated and locally of finite presentation over
k
).

4.1.1.
Perverse sheaves, mixed Hodge modules and motives

In this section, we consider the category of separated schemes of finite type over
k
, an algebraically closed field of characteristic
0
. Given a triangulated category
\mathcal{D}
with a
t
-structure with heart
\mathcal{A}
, we denote by
\mathcal{D}^{b}
,
\mathcal{D}^{+}
and
\mathcal{D}^{-}
the triangulated subcategory of objects which are bounded, resp left bounded, resp right bounded, with respect to this
t
-structure.

On a scheme
X
, we consider the derived category
D(X,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
of complexes of étale sheaves of
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
-modules over
X
. Over
k
, a field of characteristic zero with a fixed embedding
\sigma:k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, denoting by
X^{an}
the analytification of its base change to
\mathbb{C}
, we consider
D(X,\mathbb{Q})
the derived category of sheaves of
\mathbb{Q}
-modules for the analytic topology on
X^{an}
. We denote by
D_{c}(X,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
(resp.
D_{c}(X,\mathbb{Q})
), the triangulated subcategory of
D(X,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
(resp
D(X^{an},\mathbb{Q})
) of complexes with constructible homology, with constructibility considered with respect to an algebraic stratification. We consider the perverse
t
-structure (with middle perversity) built in
[
BBD82
]
, on
D_{c}(X,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
(resp.
D_{c}(X,\mathbb{Q})
), with heart denoted by
{\rm Perv}(X,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
(resp.
{\rm Perv}(X,\mathbb{Q})
), called the Abelian category of perverse sheaves. By the comparison theorem for étale cohomology (see
[
BBD82
, Section 6.1.2]
), one has that the étale and analytic constructions are equivalent (and that this equivalence commutes with the six operations introduced below) when one take the coefficient ring to be a torsion ring on the two sides. These carry a six functor formalism (valued in small categories, instead of presentable one), with all the expected properties, built in
[
VSDG72
]
(this is in fact the first construction of six functor formalism, which inspired the general formalism presented above).

There are various refinements of constructible complexes, giving for
X
for any sufficiently nice scheme over a certain base, a triangulated category
\mathcal{D}_{c}(X)
with six operations, a perverse
t
-structure with Abelian heart
\mathcal{A}
, and a faithful exact (and then conservative) functor called the Betti realization
rat_{B}:\mathcal{D}_{c}(X)\to D_{c}(X,\mathbb{Q})
(in characteristic
0
) or the
\ell
-adic realization
rat_{\ell}:\mathcal{D}_{c}(X)\to D_{c}(X,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
, commuting with the six functors. The idea is then that
\mathcal{D}_{c}(Spec(k))
contains more information on the cohomology on varieties than
D_{c}({\rm Spec}(k),\Lambda)
, which is the category of complexes of
\Lambda
-modules, and that under
rat
one keep only the information of the cohomology as a complex of vector space. We will denote by
{}^{p}\mathcal{H}^{i}
the functors giving the homology with respect to the perverse
t
-structure.

Over
\mathbb{C}
, a refinement of usual cohomology, using analytic geometry, is given by Deligne’s theory of mixed Hodge structure. A relative version of that theory, with a full six functor formalism, is given by Saito’s Abelian category of mixed Hodge module
MHM(X)
, built in
[
Sai88
]
and
[
Sai86
]
. One has a forgetful functor
rat:DMHM(X)\to D_{c}(X,\mathbb{Q})
, which is exact for the perverse
t
-structure and commutes with the six functors.

According to Grothendieck’s philosophy of mixed motives, the universal version of cohomology theory must be given by Voedvosky’s triangulated category of mixed motives. A relative version of that, with a six functors formalism, called the theory of constructible rational étale motives, denoted by
DM_{c}(X,\mathbb{Q})
, is constructed in Ayoub’s thesis
[
Ayo07a
]
,
[
Ayo07b
]
and by Cisinki and Déglise in
[
CD09
]
. It has a natural forgetful functor to
D_{c}(X,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
(resp.
D_{c}(X,\mathbb{Q})
over a field
k
of characteristic zero with a fixed embedding
\sigma:k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
) commuting with the six functors called the
\ell
-adic (resp. Betti) realization, build in
[
Ayo14
]
(resp.
[
Ayo09
]
). The existence of a perverse
t
-structure on this triangulated category compatible with the perverse
t
-structure under the forgetful functor is a really difficult conjecture, equivalent to Grothendieck’s standard conjectures (and in particular implying the Hodge conjecture).

A universal approximation of the perverse heart of such a perverse t-structure over a field over a field
k
of characteristic zero with a fixed embedding
\sigma:k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is given by Nori’s motives, and its relative version is provided by the Abelian category of perverse Nori motives
\mathcal{M}_{perv}(X)
. A six functor formalism on
D\mathcal{M}_{perv}
has been built in
[
IM23
]
and
[
Ter24
]
. It inherits a Betti and
\ell
-adic realization commuting with the six operations from
[
IM23
]
. For
X
a scheme over
\mathbb{C}
, one has from
[
Tub23
, Theorem 0.1]
a sequence of exact, faithful, perverse exact and conservative functors commuting with the six functors:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\mathcal{M}_{perv}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DMHM(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{c}(X,\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{c}(X,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})}

Notice that from
[
Ter24
]
, the perverse Nori motives a posteriori don’t depends of the embedding
\sigma:k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
(but the Betti realization depends on it), hence one can, by taking limits over subfields that are finite extensions of
\mathbb{Q}
, and then admits an embedding in
\mathbb{C}
, define perverse Nori motives for arbitrary large fields
k
of characteristic
0
. In this case, there will be no Betti realization, but we can still check that a morphism of constructible objects is an isomorphism at the Betti level using a limit argument. According to
[
Tub23
, Theorem 5.7]
, if there exists a perverse t-structure on rational étale motivic sheaves over a field
k
of characteristic
0
, then the triangulated category of mixed motivic sheaves must correspond with the derived category of perverse Nori motives.

In this section, we use then the notation
\mathcal{D}_{c},\mathcal{A}_{c}
to denote constructible complexes, mixed Hodge modules and Perverse Nori motives, with their respective heart. In addition to having a perverse
t
-structure, these six-functor formalisms satisfies the following additional properties:

•

General six functor formalisms satisfies Nisnevich descent, and then also descent along smooth morphism with Nisnevich-local sections.
\mathcal{D}_{c}
satisfies furthermore étale descent, and then also descent along any smooth morphism.

•

Consider the Tate twists
\{d\}=[2d](d):\mathcal{D}_{c}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{c}(X)

\displaystyle\{d\}=[2d](d):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Sigma^{\mathcal{O}^{d}_{X}}:&\mbox{if }d\geq 0\in E\\
\Sigma^{-\mathcal{O}^{d}_{X}}&\mbox{if }d<0\end{array}\right.

one has that
(d)
is perverse exact.
\mathcal{D}_{c}
is oriented, which means that the Thom twist
\Sigma^{\mathcal{E}}
of a locally free sheaf of rank
d
is canonically isomorphic with
\{d\}
. We slightly abuse the notation, by using also the notation
\{d\}
for
d
a locally constant function on
X
. The purity isomorphism gives then, for
f
smooth of relative dimension
d_{f}
, a natural isomorphism
f^{!}\simeq f^{\ast}\{d_{f}\}
. Moreover,
f^{\ast}[d_{f}]
is perverse-exact. Notice that we slightly abuse the notation here: indeed,
d_{f}
is defined on the domain of
f
, so we use the shorthand notation
f^{\ast}\{d_{f}\}
(resp.
f^{\ast}[d_{f}]
) for
\{d_{f}\}\circ f^{\ast}
(resp
[d_{f}]\circ f^{\ast}
).

•

Moreover, these affords a formalism of Verdier duality (such a formalism was developed for arbitrary motivic coefficient system in
[
Ayo07a
, Section 2.3.10]
, using constructibility assumptions). Namely, there is a contravariant involution
\mathbb{D}_{X}:\mathbb{D}(X)^{op}\to\mathbb{D}(X)
, commuting with the exterior tensor product, and exchanging
!
and
\ast
functors. Namely, for
f:X\to Y
, there are natural equivalences
\mathbb{D}_{X}f^{!}\simeq f^{\ast}\mathbb{D}_{Y}
and
\mathbb{D}_{X}f_{\ast}\simeq f_{!}\mathbb{D}_{Y}
, compatible with composition and base change.

4.1.2.
The formalism of weights

One of the main advantages of the refinements of the derived category of constructible complexes considered here is the formalism of weights, inspired by the formalism of weights for mixed
\ell
-adic sheaves. Namely, for mixed Hodge modules and perverse Nori motives, one has for each
w\in\mathbb{Z}
a full semisimple subcategory of the perverse heart, called the category of pure objects of weight
w
, and general objects
F
of the perverse heart has a canonical increasing weight filtration
W_{\bullet}F
such that the
Gr^{W}_{w}F
is pure of weight
w
, and the morphisms are strict for the weight filtration.

For mixed
\ell
-adic perverse sheaves, purity is defined in
[
BBD82
]
by considering eigenvalues of the Frobenius, and the weight filtration is built in the definition. For mixed Hodge modules, pure objects are the polarizable Hodge modules defined in
[
Sai88
]
, and the weight filtration is built in the definition in
[
Sai86
]
. A weight structure on perverse Nori motives is built by Ivorra and Morel in
[
IM23
]
using the realization to
\ell
-adic sheaves and the Bondarko weight structure on étale motivic sheaves built by Bondarko
[
Bon10
]
and Hébert
[
Héb10
]
. By construction, the Bondarko weight structure on étale motivic sheaves is compatible with the Saito’s weight structure on MHM under the Hodge realization, hence Ivorra-Morel weight structure on perverse Nori motives is also compatible with Saito’s one under the Hodge realization.

These weight structure extends to the derived category
\mathcal{D}_{c}(X)
. Namely, one consider
\mathcal{D}_{c,\leq w}(X)
(resp
\mathcal{D}_{c,\geq w}(X)
,
\mathcal{D}_{c,w}(X)
), the subcategories of objects
F\in\mathcal{D}_{c}(X)
such that, for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,
{}^{p}\mathcal{H}^{i}(F)
has weights less than or equal to
w+i
(resp more than or equal to
w+i
, resp are pure of weight
w+i
). In all of these cases the category
\mathcal{D}_{c,w}(X)
of pure objects are semi-simple, and these define weight structure in the sense of Bondarko
[
Bon07
]
.

The six functor formalism interacts well with weights. Namely, for
f:X\to Y
a morphism, one obtains for each
w\in\mathbb{Z}
:

(4.3)

\displaystyle f^{\ast}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{D}_{c,\leq w}(Y)\to\mathcal{D}_{c,\leq w}(X)

(4.4)

\displaystyle f^{!}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{D}_{c,\geq w}(Y)\to\mathcal{D}_{c,\geq w}(X)

(4.5)

\displaystyle f_{\ast}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{D}_{c,\geq w}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{c,\geq w}(Y)

(4.6)

\displaystyle f_{!}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{D}_{c,\leq w}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{c,\leq w}(Y)

moreover, weights are additive under the exterior tensor product, and
\mathbb{D}
exchange
\mathcal{D}_{c,\leq w}
and
\mathcal{D}_{c,\geq-w}
. Moreover, the Tate twist
(1)
shifts the weight by
-2
(such that
\{1\}=[2](1)
preserves
\mathcal{D}_{c,w}(X)
).

In particular, for
f:X\to Y
proper, one obtains an analogue of Beilinson-Bernstein-Deligne-Gabber decomposition theorem from
[
BBD82
]
. Namely, for
F
pure of weight
w
in
\mathcal{A}(X)
,
f_{\ast}F
is pure of weight
w
, and then one has a direct sum decomposition

(4.7)

\displaystyle f_{\ast}F=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}^{p}\mathcal{H}^{i}f_{\ast}F[-i]=\bigoplus_{j}G_{j}[-n_{j}]

with
G_{j}
being simple objects, pure of weight
w+n_{j}
in the perverse heart.

Denoting
p:X\to pt
the projection to a point, we consider the refining of the cohomology with compact support of
X
:

(4.8)

\displaystyle H^{i}_{c}(X)=^{p}\mathcal{H}^{i}p_{!}p^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}_{pt}\in\mathcal{A}

one find that if
X
is smooth and proper, then
H^{i}_{c}(X)
has weights
i
, which corresponds to Grothendieck’s idea that smooth and proper varieties corresponds to pure motives.

4.1.3.
Extension to stacks

In this section, all our stacks are assumed to be locally of finite type, over an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic
0
.

The extension of six functor formalism for constructible complexes to stacks was done in
[
LZ12
]
,
[
LZ12
]
, and for mixed Hodge modules and perverse Nori motives in
[
Tub24
]
. In
[
Tub24
]
, Tubach gives moreover the compatibility of this construction to other approaches to defining mixed Hodge modules on stacks, mainly in the quotient case. Two main technical issues must be taken into account to applies the general descent technique as discussed in Section
2.1.2
:

•

\mathcal{D}_{c}
is traditionally provided with a
2
-categorical version of a six functor formalism, instead of an
\infty
-categorical one. But, as discussed in Section
2.1.2
, one must really have an
\infty
-categorical enhancement to do descent at the level of the derived category. Constructible complexes are defined as étale (or analytic) sheaves, hence the operations on them admits naturally an
\infty
-categorical enhancement, constructed in
[
LZ12
]
. However, the construction of mixed Hodge modules and their six functors by Saito in
[
Sai88
]
and
[
Sai86
]
is really intricate, hence defining them in a homotopy coherent way could seem hopeless. Tubach has shown in
[
Tub23
]
that the six functors on mixed Hodge modules and perverse Nori motives admits a natural
\infty
-categorical enhancement, which was the main missing piece. The main idea was to consider an other
t
-structure on
D^{b}MHM
and
D^{b}\mathcal{M}_{perv}
, send to the classical (not perverse)
t
-structure of
D^{b}_{c}(-,\mathbb{Q})
, and to show that
D^{b}MHM
and
D^{b}\mathcal{M}_{perv}
are the derived category of this heart. The functors are then half exact with respect to this new heart, hence where the derived functor of functors of half-exact functors of Abelian categories, which admits naturally an
\infty
-categorical enhancement.

•

\mathcal{D}_{c}
is valued in small categories, instead of presentable categories, hence arbitrary colimits do not exists, which prevents the use of descent. One need then to embed
\mathcal{D}_{c}
into a presentable categories: one consider the category of Ind-objects of
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}
, and applies Liu-Zengh descent to it. One define then
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{X})
to be the full subcategory of objects whose pullbacks to covering scheme
X
are in
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(X)
. Recall that, for
f
smooth,
f^{\ast}[d_{f}]
is perverse exact, which allows to define by smooth descent a perverse
t
-structure on the big category. Now,
\mathcal{D}_{c}(\mathcal{X})
is defined to be the full subcategory whose objects have cohomology in
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(X)
with respect to this
t
-structure. One obtains then a stable
(\infty,1)
-category
\mathcal{D}_{c}(\mathcal{X})
, with a perverse
t
srtructure with heart
\mathcal{A}_{c}(\mathcal{X})
, and subcategories of (left-, right-) bounded objects
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{X})
(
\mathcal{D}^{+}_{c}(\mathcal{X})
,
\mathcal{D}^{-}_{c}(\mathcal{X})
).

The main result of the construction is given in
[
LZ12
, Proposition 6.4.4, 6.4.5]
and
[
Tub24
, Theorem 3.8]
. One has the following subtleties:

•

\mathcal{D}_{c}(X)
is a triangulated category with a perverse
t
-structure which is in general not the derived category of its Abelian perverse heart (for schemes, it was the case by definition for mixed Hodge modules and perverse Nori motives, and this was proven for constructible complexes in
[
Bei87
]
).

•

By descent, for any
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
,
f^{\ast},f^{!}
preserves
\mathcal{D}^{?}_{c}
. As usual when one deals with constructible objects, only pushforward by morphisms of finite type can preserve them. If
f
is representable by algebraic spaces and of finite type,
f_{\ast}
and
f_{!}
preserves
\mathcal{D}^{?}_{c}
, by descent from the scheme case. If
f
is of finite type (possibly not representable), one need to use a spectral sequence argument, and one obtains only:

\displaystyle f_{\ast}:\mathcal{D}^{+}_{c}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathcal{D}^{+}_{c}(\mathcal{Y})

(4.9)

\displaystyle f_{!}:\mathcal{D}^{-}_{c}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathcal{D}^{-}_{c}(\mathcal{Y})

for example, equivariant (resp. compact equivariant) cohomology can be unbounded in the positive (resp negative) direction.

•

The orientability gives that Thom twists by any perfect complexes in amplitude
[0,1]
are naturally equivalent with Tate twists by their virtual dimension. In particular, for
f
smooth of virtual relative dimension
d_{f}
, the purity gives a natural isomorphism
f^{!}\simeq f^{\ast}\{d_{f}\}
.

•

From
[
Tub24
, Section 3.2]
, the Verdier duality operation extends to stacks, fix
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}
and exchanges
\mathcal{D}^{+}_{c}
with
\mathcal{D}^{-}_{c}
, commutes with
\boxtimes
, and exchanges
!
and
\ast
functors, as expected.

•

In the mixed Hodge module and perverse Nori motives case, from
[
Tub24
, Section 3.3]
, there is a weight structure in the sense of Bondarko which is defined on
\mathcal{D}_{c}(\mathcal{X})
for
\mathcal{X}
an algebraic stack with affine stabilizers. In this case, the functors
f^{\ast},f^{!},f_{\ast},f_{!},\mathbb{D},\boxtimes
have the expected behavior with respect to weights. Notice that one can define naturally a notion of weights for any algebraic stacks, but this will in general not provide a weight structure in the sense of Bondarko (namely, pure objects will not be always semisimple), and pushforward from stacks with stabilizers which are not affine will not always have the good functoriality with weights. See the example of
BE
, with
E
an elliptic curve group, studied in the mixed
\ell
-adic context in
[
Sun12
]
. In this paper, we will consider only stacks with affine stabilizers (as only those appear in generalized GIT problem), hence we will not have to deal with those subtleties.

Braden-Drinfeld-Gaitsgory theorem on stacks
3.1
has then the following consequence, in the presence of a weight structure:

Corollary 4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an algebraic stack, locally of finite type over
k
, with affine stabilizers. If
\mathcal{D}_{c}(-)
denotes the extension of mixed Hodge modules or perverse Nori motives to stacks, then the hyperbolic localization functor:

(4.10)

\displaystyle p_{!}\eta^{\ast}\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}({\rm Grad}(\mathcal{X}))

is weight exact, i.e. preserves pure objects.

Proof.

Theorem
3.1
gives an isomorphism between
p_{!}\eta^{\ast}
, which preserves
\mathcal{D}^{+}_{c,\geq w}
and
(r\circ p)_{\ast}\eta^{!}
, which preserves
\mathcal{D}^{-}_{c,\leq w}
.
∎

This corollary was the main motivation of Braden in
[
Bra02
]
to establish his main result, in the case of schemes with
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action. It allows to interpret (
3.41
) as a decomposition theorem.

4.2.
Vanishing cycles and monodromic objects

4.2.1.
Vanishing cycles

Consider
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}=\mathbb{C}-\{0\}
, its universal cover
\tilde{\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}
(notice that this universal cover is the exponential map, which is not algebraic, hence one really use analytic geometry here!). For any
\mathbb{C}
-algebraic space
X
, and regular map
X\to\mathbb{C}
, considering its analytification
X^{an}
, one consider the Cartesian diagram obtained by base change:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{X^{an}_{\eta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{an}_{\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{an}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

A deep result of
[
BBD82
]
is that the functor:

(4.11)

\displaystyle i^{\ast}j_{\ast}\tilde{\theta}_{\ast}\tilde{\theta}^{\ast}:D(X^{an}_{\eta},\mathbb{Q})\to D(X_{0}^{an},\mathbb{Q})

restricts to a functor
\psi_{f}^{an}:D^{b}_{c}(X_{\eta},\mathbb{Q})\to D^{b}_{c}(X_{0},\mathbb{Q})
, exact for the perverse
t
-structure up to a shift by
[1]
, called the nearby cycle functors. The problem in this definition is that the universal cover of
\tilde{\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}
is given by the exponential map, which is not algebraic, hence one has to go into the analytic world to do this definition. In the étale world, i.e. for
D(X,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
, nearby cycles are defined in
[
GDK72
, Exposé XIII]
: namely, one consider the spectrum
S
of the strict Henselianization of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
at
0
(a kind of local ring for the étale topology), with one generic point
\eta
(the étale local version of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
) and one point
s
.
\eta
is now the spectrum of a field, and has then an universal cover
\bar{\eta}
, given by the spectrum of a separable closure: one can then copy the above definition, using the base change
X_{\bar{\eta}}
instead of
\tilde{X^{an}_{\eta}}
. The comparison theorem of
[
GDK72
, Exposé XIV]
shows that the étale and analytic definitions agrees for a torsion coefficient ring. However, this definition does not work using the coefficient ring
\mathbb{Q}
, as explained in
[
AM07
, Page 12]
.

A definition of nearby cycles, which works for any coefficient system, and admits a direct extension to algebraic stacks, is provided by the definition of Ayoub given in
[
Ayo07b
, Section 3.5]
, and was used in
[
IM23
]
to define nearby cycles for perverse Nori motives on schemes. An equivalent definition, more conceptual and easier to use in
(\infty,1)
categorical setting, was introduced in
[
CvdHS24
]
, which was shown to be equivalent to the topological definition for schemes, is used by Tubach in
[
Tub24
, Section 3.4]
to define the nearby cycles for mixed Hodge modules on stacks, and Tubach shows in
[
Tub24
, Proposition 3.33]
that this definition is equivalent to Saito’s definition from
[
Sai86
]
in the scheme case (up to a shift, because Saito’s functor is perverse exact). Notice that these definitions gives a specialization system
\psi_{f}:\mathcal{D}_{c}(\mathcal{X}_{\eta})\to\mathcal{D}_{c}(\mathcal{X}_{0})
exact for the perverse heart up to a shift by
[1]
by reducing to the scheme case using a smooth presentation and by using the Betti realization .

There is a natural morphism of specialization system
i^{\ast}\to\psi_{f}j^{\ast}
: one want to define
\phi_{f}
as the cone of this functor, but the cone construction is not canonical in a triangulated category. Fortunately, using the fact that these constructions have an
(\infty,1)
-categorical enhancement, one can work at the level of stable
(\infty,1)
categories, where one has a functor
{\rm Cofib}()
, sending a morphism to a cone of it in a functorial way so we can define canonically
\phi_{f}:={\rm Cofib}(i^{\ast}\to\psi_{f})
. They fits then in a natural cofiber sequence of specialization systems:

(4.12)

\displaystyle i^{\ast}\to\psi_{f}\to\phi_{f}\to i^{\ast}[1]

one obtains by reducing to the scheme case and the Betti or
\ell
-adic realization that
\phi_{f}:\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{X}_{0})
is perverse exact up to a shift by
[1]
.

4.2.2.
Six functors for monodromic objects

In
[
BBD+15
, Section 2.9]
,
[
DM16
, Section 2.1]
, the authors works with monodromic mixed Hodge modules over schemes, as suggested by
[
KS10
, Section 7.4]
. The main reason for that is that the Thom-Sebastiani theorem from
[
ST71
]
,
[
Mas01
]
, giving the compatibility of vanishing cycles with product in the setting of analytic sheaves, does not extends directly to the Hodge,
\ell
-adic or motivic level, as was observed by Deligne in
[
Del88
]
. The problem arise in the computation of vanishing cycles of quadratic functions in
[
GDK72
, Exposé XV]
: the vanishing cycle of
x^{2}+y^{2}:\mathbb{A}^{2}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
have weight
2
, which would suggest that the vanishing cycles of
x^{2}
would be one dimensional with weight
1
, which would is impossible! Deligne has suggested to avoid this by considering the vanishing cycles with the data of the monodromy as objects on
X_{0}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
, extended by
0
from
X_{0}\times\mathbb{G}_{m,k}
, where the
\mathbb{G}_{m,k}
factor encodes the monodromy. Then Deligne’s idea was that Thom-Sebastiani theorem must holds by replacing the product by the convolution product:

(4.13)

\displaystyle\boxtimes_{mon}:=\mu_{\ast}(-\boxtimes-)

where
\mu:\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
denotes the sum. The proof in the
\ell
-adic setting was written down (30 years later!) by Illusie in
[
Ill17
]
, and was given in the Hodge setting by Saito in the preprint
[
Sai10
]
.

In this paper, we need an extension of this theory to Artin stacks, and also for perverse Nori motives. Moreover, we need several compatibility results that are difficult to find in the literature. For this reason, we will detail this construction. In
[
Des24
]
, we have developed a formalism of monodromic objects on stacks, with six operations, and proven a Thom-Sebastiani theorem, for more general coefficient system. Here, things will be easier, because we can use the conservativity of the Betti realization to show that the morphisms that we define are isomorphisms, so we will be more sketchy.

We first explain how to define the cofficient system
\mathcal{D}_{mon}
. We begin with the coefficient system
\mathcal{D}
(the Ind-completion of the coefficient system
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}
we are interested in). We apply the dual of the construction of
[
GL22
]
to obtain a coefficient system
\mathcal{D}_{\exp,\ast}
, which give using
4.1.3
a six functor formalism of stacks, such that:

•

\mathcal{D}_{\exp,\ast}(\mathcal{X})
is the full
(\infty,1)
-stable subcategory of
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
whose objects
F
satisfies
\pi_{\ast}F\simeq 0
, where
\pi:\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathcal{X}
is the first projection. The inclusion functor
\mathcal{D}_{\exp,\ast}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
admits a left adjoint
\Pi_{\ast}:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\to\mathcal{D}_{\exp,\ast}(\mathcal{X})
, given by the formula
\Pi_{\ast}:={\rm Cofib}(\pi^{\ast}\pi_{\ast}\to Id)
. Notice that, by base change, the morphism
\pi_{\ast}\pi^{\ast}\to Id
is identified with
\mu_{\ast}(-\boxtimes\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}})\to\mu_{\ast}(-\boxtimes 0_{!}\mathbbm{1}_{0})
, i.e. , considering the open immersion
j:\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{G}_{m,k}\to\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
and the localization triangle
j_{!}j^{\ast}\to Id\to i_{!}i^{\ast}
, we can define as usual
\Pi_{\ast}:=\mu_{\ast}(-\boxtimes j_{!}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}[1])
.

•

Given
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
,
f^{!}:\mathcal{D}_{\exp,\ast}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{D}_{\exp,\ast}(\mathcal{X})
(resp.
f_{\ast}:\mathcal{D}_{\exp,\ast}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathcal{D}_{\exp,\ast}(\mathcal{Y})
) is obtained by restricting
(f\times Id_{\mathbb{A}^{1}})^{!}:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
(resp.
(f\times Id_{\mathbb{A}^{1}})_{\ast}:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
).
f^{\ast}:\mathcal{D}_{\exp,\ast}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{D}_{\exp,\ast}(\mathcal{X})
(resp.
f_{!}:\mathcal{D}_{\exp,\ast}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathcal{D}_{\exp,\ast}(\mathcal{Y})
) is obtained by restricting
\Pi_{\ast}(f\times Id_{\mathbb{A}^{1}})^{\ast}:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
(resp.
\Pi_{\ast}(f\times Id_{\mathbb{A}^{1}})_{!}:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
).

•

The exterior tensor product
\boxtimes_{exp}:\mathcal{D}_{\exp,\ast}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathcal{D}_{\exp,\ast}(\mathcal{Y})\mathcal{D}_{\exp,\ast}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{D}_{\exp,\ast}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})
is obtained by restriction from:

(4.14)

\displaystyle\mu_{\ast}(-\boxtimes-):\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\times\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})

(one uses here
\pi_{\ast}\mu_{\ast}(-\boxtimes-)\simeq\pi_{\ast}\boxtimes\pi_{\ast}
).

More precisely, in
[
GL22
]
, the authors define an operation called exponentiation, sending a coefficient system
\mathcal{D}
to a coefficient system
\mathcal{D}_{exp,!}
with the above properties, but where
!
and
\ast
functors, and the direction of the arrows are switched. We use here a trick from
[
Ayo07a
, Page 211]
: from the six functor formalism
\mathcal{D}^{\ast}_{!}
, we obtain by passing to the adjoints a functor
(\mathcal{D}^{!})^{op}
, which satisfies all the axioms of a coefficient system. Then, we apply the following sequence of constructions:

(4.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\to(\mathcal{D}^{!})^{op}\to((\mathcal{D}^{!})^{op})_{exp,!}\to((((\mathcal{D}^{!})^{op})_{exp,!})^{!})^{op}=:\mathcal{D}_{exp,\ast}

where the middle row is the construction of
[
GL22
]
.

We consider then the full sub
(\infty,1)
-categories
\mathcal{D}_{mon}(\mathcal{X})
of
\mathcal{D}_{exp,!}(\mathcal{X})
whose objects are
F\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
such that
\pi_{\ast}F=0
which are moreover monodromic, i.e. such that for each
x\in\mathcal{X}(k)
,
F|_{\{x\}\times\mathbb{G}_{m,k}}
has Betti realization whose cohomology is locally constant, and we denote by
\mathcal{D}^{?}_{mon,c}(\mathcal{X}):=\mathcal{D}_{mon}(\mathcal{X})\cap\mathcal{D}^{?}_{c}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
the full subcategory of constructible objects. From the definition, using base change, for any
f
,
f^{\ast},f_{!}
preserve monodromic objects. It is standard that
\mathbb{D}
and
\mu_{\ast}(-\boxtimes-)
preserve monodromic objects in the scheme case, hence the same follows in the stack case by smooth descent: in particular,
f^{!},f_{\ast}
also preserve monodromic objects. As the image of
\pi^{\ast}
is obviously made of monodromic objects,
\Pi_{\ast}:={\rm Cofib}(\pi^{\ast}\pi_{\ast}\to Id)
obviously preserves monodromic objects. Hence the six operations on
\mathcal{D}_{exp,\ast}
preserves
\mathcal{D}_{mon}
, such that we obtain by restriction a six functor formalism
\mathcal{D}_{mon}
: we denote the restriction of
\boxtimes_{exp}
by
\boxtimes_{mon}
, or simply
\boxtimes
when the fact that we work with monodromic objects is clear from the context.

From
[
Ver81
, Lemme 6.1, Section 9]
, for schemes in the Betti case, the natural morphism
\pi_{\ast}\to\pi_{\ast}0_{\ast}0^{\ast}\simeq 0^{\ast}
is an isomorphism when applied to monodromic objects, hence the same applies for stacks and
\mathcal{D}
by conservativity of the Betti realization and smooth pullbacks. Then
\mathcal{D}_{mon}(\mathcal{X})
is also the full subcategory of objects
F\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
who are monodromic and such that
0^{\ast}F\simeq 0
. In particular, the natural morphisms
f^{\ast}\to\Pi_{\ast}f^{\ast}
and
f_{!}\to\Pi_{\ast}f_{!}
are isomorphisms when applied to objects of
\mathcal{D}_{mon}
, hence the four functors
f^{\ast},f^{!},f_{\ast},f_{!}
on
\mathcal{D}_{mon}
are obtained by restricting
(f\times Id_{\mathbb{A}^{1}})^{\ast},(f\times Id_{\mathbb{A}^{1}})^{!},(f\times Id_{\mathbb{A}^{1}})_{\ast},(f\times Id_{\mathbb{A}^{1}})_{!})
on
\mathcal{D}(-\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
.

By definition, the perverse
t
-structure on
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
restricts to the full subcategory of monodromic objects, and, on this
\Pi_{\ast}
preserve the perverse heart (it is standard for schemes in the Betti case, hence it follows for stacks and
\mathcal{D}
using smooth pullbacks and the Betti realization), hence this perverse
t
-structure restricts to a perverse
t
-structure on
\mathcal{D}_{mon}
(notice that this is false for
\mathcal{D}_{exp,\ast}
). Then
\boxtimes_{mon}
is perverse exact (it is standard for schemes in the Betti case, hence it follows for stacks and
\mathcal{D}
using smooth pullbacks and the Betti realization), and the four functors have the usual properties with respect to the perverse heart (in particular, for
f
smooth of relative dimension
d_{f}
,
f^{\ast}[d_{f}]
is perverse exact).

Denoting
q:\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}\to\mathcal{X}
, the functor
\Pi_{\ast}0_{\ast}\simeq j_{!}q^{\ast}[1]:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}_{mon}
is fully faithful, perverse-exact and commutes with the six functors: it associates an object to the corresponding monodromic object with trivial monodromy. Its quasi-inverse
1^{\ast}[-1]:\mathcal{D}_{mon}\to\mathcal{D}
, the operation of forgetting the monodromy, is perverse exact and faithful (indeed, it is known to be perverse exact and faithful for schemes in the Betti case, hence the same follows for stacks and
\mathcal{D}
by perverse-exactness faithfullness of smooth pullback and the Betti realization), it commutes with the operations
f^{\ast},f^{!},f_{\ast},f_{!}
, but not with
\boxtimes
(otherwise, the non-monodromic version of Thom-Sebastiani would be true).

4.2.3.
Thom-Sebastiani theorem

Following
[
KS10
]
, for
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
, we consider
f/t:\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{G}_{m,k}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
, with
t
the coordinate of
\mathbb{G}_{m,k}
, and define the monodromic vanishing cycles and total monodromic vanishing cycles to be:

\displaystyle\phi_{f}^{mon}

\displaystyle:=(j_{0})_{!}\phi_{f/t}q^{\ast}:\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{X}_{0}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})

(4.16)

\displaystyle\phi_{f}^{mon,tot}

\displaystyle:=\bigoplus_{c\in k}(i_{c})_{\ast}\phi_{f-c}^{mon}:\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})

These are perverse exact, because
\phi_{f}[-1]
,
q^{\ast}[1]
,
j_{!}
and
(i_{c})_{!}
are perverse exact. As
\phi_{f}
enhance to a specialization system
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}\to\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}
over
\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}\leftarrow\{0\}
,
\phi_{f}^{mon}
(resp.
\phi_{f}^{mon,tot}
) enhance to a specialization
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}\to\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}
over
\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}\overset{0}{\leftarrow}\mathbb{A}^{1}
(resp.
\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}\overset{pr_{1}}{\leftarrow}\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
. The Betti realization is monodromic in the scheme case from
[
Ver81
, Proposition 7.1, Section 9]
, hence the same follows for stacks by smooth pullbacks. Then, as the four operations
f^{\ast},f^{!},f_{\ast},f_{!}
on
\mathcal{D}_{mon}
are induced by the four operations for
(f\times Id_{\mathbb{A}^{1}})^{\ast},(f\times Id_{\mathbb{A}^{1}})^{!},(f\times Id_{\mathbb{A}^{1}})_{\ast},(f\times Id_{\mathbb{A}^{1}})_{!})
on
\mathcal{D}(-\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
, those restricts to perverse-exact specialization systems:

\displaystyle\phi_{f}^{mon}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathcal{D}^{b}_{mon,c}(\mathcal{X}_{0})

(4.17)

\displaystyle\phi_{f}^{mon,tot}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{D}^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathcal{D}^{b}_{mon,c}(\mathcal{X})

over
\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}\leftarrow\{0\}
(resp.
\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}\leftarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
). We recall that this gives, for
g:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
, natural morphisms:

\displaystyle g^{\ast}\phi_{f}^{mon,tot}\to\phi_{f\circ g}^{mon,tot}g^{\ast}

(4.18)

\displaystyle\phi_{f\circ g}^{mon,tot}g^{!}\to g^{!}\phi_{f}^{mon,tot}

which are inverse isomorphisms up to a Tate twist if
g
is smooth, and:

\displaystyle g_{!}\phi_{f\circ g}^{mon,tot}\to\phi_{f}^{mon,tot}g_{!}

(4.19)

\displaystyle\phi_{f}^{mon,tot}g_{\ast}\to g_{\ast}\phi_{f\circ g}^{mon,tot}

which are inverse isomorphisms if
g
is proper, with compatibility with base change and composition (and similarly for
\phi^{mon}
). Notice that there is a natural morphism of specialization system:

(4.20)

\displaystyle 1^{\ast}[-1]\phi^{mon}_{f}:=1^{\ast}[-1](j_{0})_{!}\phi_{f/t}q^{\ast}\simeq 1^{\ast}\phi_{f/t}q^{\ast}[-1]\to\phi_{f}1^{\ast}q^{\ast}[-1]\simeq\phi_{f}[-1]

it is an isomorphism for schemes in the Betti case from
[
Ver81
, Proposition 7.1, Section 9]
, hence it is also an isomorphism for stacks and
\mathcal{D}
by conservativity. Hence the monodromic vanishing cycle is send to the classical (perverse) vanishing cycle under the forgetful functor.

With these definitions in hand, we can extend the construction of Verdier
[
Ver81
]
, Deligne and Illusie
[
Ill17
]
, and Saito
[
Sai10
]
to stacks and perverse Nori motives:

Theorem 4.2
.

For
k
an algebraically closed fields of characteristic
0
,
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
,
g:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
morphisms from locally of finite type Artin
1
-stacks over
k
, there is a natural isomorphism of specialization systems:

(4.21)

\displaystyle\phi^{mon,tot}_{f}\boxtimes_{mon}\phi^{mon,tot}_{g}\simeq\phi^{mon,tot}_{f\boxplus g}(-\boxtimes-):\mathcal{D}_{c}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathcal{D}_{c}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{D}_{mon,c}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})

(in particular, it is compatible with the morphisms (
4.2.3
) and (
4.2.3
)). These isomorphisms satisfies the obvious commutativity and associativity property.

Proof.
